On the left, a central vertex $v$ is connected to vertices $u_1, u_2, u_3$ via edges $e_1, e_2, e_3$, where $e_1$ is drawn as a thick blue line and $e_2, e_3$ are thin black lines. A horizontal arrow labelled "expand" points to the right configuration. On the right, vertex $v$ is expanded into a triangle with vertices $t_1, t_2, t_3$, connected to $u_1, u_2, u_3$ by edges $e_1, e_2, e_3$, with $e_1$ shown as a thick blue line. Inside the triangle, the edge opposite to $t_1$ between $t_2$ and $t_3$ is labelled $f_1$ and shown as a thick blue line; the other two edges of the triangle ($t_1 t_2$ and $t_1 t_3$) are thin black lines.


<div class="samepage">

<a id="stmt-ex-lem-expansion-face"></a>
<div id="ex:lem-expansion-face" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16** (The face of a triangle expansion). *The set $$Q_T=\{y\in P(G'):y(\delta(T))=1\}$$ is a face of $P(G')$ affinely isomorphic to $P(G)$. Under this isomorphism an old edge keeps its coordinate and $$y_{f_i}=y_{e_i}\qquad(1\le i\le3).$$ If $x\in P(G)$ has coordinate $1/3$ on every edge, its image $x'$ has coordinate $1/3$ on every edge of $G'$. The isomorphism maps the minimal face at $x$ onto the minimal face at $x'$.*

</div>

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ex-lem-expansion-face"><summary>Proof of stmt-ex-lem-expansion-face</summary>

*Proof.* Every perfect matching of $G'$ crosses the odd cut $\delta(T)$ either once or three times. Hence $y(\delta(T))\ge1$ is valid on $P(G')$, and its equality set is a face. A convex combination lies in this face exactly when every matching with positive coefficient crosses the cut once. For these matchings the extension and contraction just described are inverse bijections, and the indicator of $f_i$ equals the indicator of $e_i$. They therefore induce the stated affine isomorphism, with inverse given by restriction to the old edge labels.

The coordinate statement follows directly. Finally, $Q_T$ is a face containing $x'$. The minimal face of $P(G')$ at $x'$ is therefore also the minimal face of $Q_T$ at $x'$; affine isomorphisms preserve minimal faces. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### A triangular minimal face for every order

Begin with two vertices joined by three distinct labelled parallel edges. Its three one-edge perfect matchings partition its edge set. Repeatedly expand any vertex as above. Each expansion adds two vertices and three edges, and all degrees remain three. Denote any graph obtained after $m-1$ expansions by $G_m$, so that $$|V(G_m)|=2m,\qquad |E(G_m)|=3m.$$ The three initial perfect matchings extend uniquely at each step. Their extensions $M_1,M_2,M_3$ still partition the edge set: the old edges retain their partition, and $f_i$ belongs to the extension of the matching containing $e_i$. In particular, $$x^{(m)}=\tfrac13\bigl(a(M_1)+a(M_2)+a(M_3)\bigr)$$ has every coordinate equal to $1/3$ and belongs to the full perfect-matching polytope.

The first expansion gives $K_4$; subsequent expansions preserve simplicity. Thus $G_m$ is simple for every $m\ge2$, and these are Klee graphs. Only the starting case $m=1$ needs parallel edges.

<a id="stmt-ex-prop-klee-face"></a>
<div id="ex:prop-klee-face" class="proposition">

<a id="ex:eq-klee-values"></a>
**Proposition 17** (Sharp codimension and prescribed-marginal entropy). *For every integer $m\ge1$, the graph $G_m$ and the point $x^{(m)}$ above satisfy $$F_{x^{(m)}}=
 \operatorname{conv}\{a(M_1),a(M_2),a(M_3)\},
 \qquad d(x^{(m)})=2.$$ The unique law on perfect matchings with mean $x^{(m)}$ is the uniform law on $M_1,M_2,M_3$. Consequently, $$\label{ex:eq-klee-values}
 \begin{aligned}
 |E(G_m)|-d(x^{(m)})&=3m-2,\\
 H(x^{(m)})&=\ln3,\\
 F(x^{(m)})&=m\ln3,\\
 B(x^{(m)})&=2m\ln(3/2).
 \end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ex-prop-klee-face"><summary>Proof of stmt-ex-prop-klee-face</summary>

*Proof.* For $m=1$, the three matching vectors are the three coordinate vectors in $\mathbb R^3$, and $P(G_1)$ is their simplex. Its uniform point is in its relative interior. Repeated application of Lemma <a href="#ex:lem-expansion-face" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:lem-expansion-face">16</a> shows that the minimal face at $x^{(m)}$ is the image of this simplex, with vertices $a(M_1),a(M_2),a(M_3)$. In particular its dimension is two.

One can also see the restriction on laws directly. For the last inserted triangle, a law with edge marginals $1/3$ has expected cut-crossing count $3(1/3)=1$. Since each matching crosses this cut once or three times, it must cross once almost surely. Contraction gives a law on the preceding graph with the same uniform marginals, and the crossing-one extension is unique. Iterating leaves only the three initial matchings. Thus the original law is supported on $M_1,M_2,M_3$. Since these matchings partition the edges, the marginal of any edge in $M_i$ equals the probability assigned to $M_i$, which must be $1/3$. This proves uniqueness and the entropy formula.

There are $3m$ edges, all with marginal $1/3$. Substitution in the definitions of $F$ and $B$ gives the remaining formulas. ◻

</details>

All coordinates here are strictly between zero and one. Equation <a href="#ex:eq-klee-values" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ex:eq-klee-values">[ex:eq-klee-values]</a> therefore attains the bound in Proposition <a href="#geom:prop-rank" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:prop-rank">6</a> for every $m\ge1$. Equivalently, by Equation <a href="#geom:eq-rank" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:eq-rank">[geom:eq-rank]</a>, the tight odd-cut rows have rank $3m-2$. The dimension two is the dimension of the minimal face at $x^{(m)}$; the whole perfect-matching polytope can have additional vertices.

<a id="stmt-ex-cor-coefficient"></a>
<div id="ex:cor-coefficient" class="corollary">

**Corollary 18** (Restriction on a universal entropy coefficient). *If a constant $c$ satisfies $$H(x)\ge F(x)-cB(x)$$ for every finite loopless multigraph $G$ and every $x\in P(G)$, then $$c\ge\frac{\ln3}{2\ln(3/2)}.$$ The same necessary bound holds if the assertion is restricted to simple graphs.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ex-cor-coefficient"><summary>Proof of stmt-ex-cor-coefficient</summary>

*Proof.* Applying the asserted inequality to $G_m,x^{(m)}$ gives $$c\ge
 \frac{(m-1)\ln3}{2m\ln(3/2)}.$$ Let $m$ tend to infinity. The graphs with $m\ge2$ are simple, so the same limit applies under that restriction. ◻

</details>

The necessary coefficient in Corollary <a href="#ex:cor-coefficient" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ex:cor-coefficient">18</a> lies strictly between $1$ and the universal coefficient $2$ supplied by Theorem <a href="#ent:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ent:main">1</a>. Sharpness of the geometric estimate therefore does not settle the optimal entropy coefficient. The next example makes the failure of coefficient one explicit at a fixed order.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### An eight-vertex obstruction to coefficient one

For a concrete instance, take vertex set $\{0,1,\ldots,7\}$ and edge set $$E=\{01,03,05,14,16,23,24,27,34,56,57,67\},$$ where $ij$ denotes the undirected edge with endpoints $i,j$. Contracting the triangles $T=\{2,3,4\}$ and $U=\{5,6,7\}$ gives $K_4$. Thus this graph is obtained by expanding two vertices of $K_4$, and is an instance of $G_4$.

Its five perfect matchings are $$\begin{aligned}
 M_1&=\{01,27,34,56\},&
 M_2&=\{03,16,24,57\},\\
 M_3&=\{05,14,23,67\},&
 N_1&=\{03,14,27,56\},\\
 &&N_2&=\{05,16,27,34\}.
 \end{aligned}$$ This list follows without enumeration software. A matching uses one of $01,03,05$. If it uses $01$, the remaining two odd triangles are joined only by $27$, forcing $M_1$. If it uses $03$, vertex $1$ is matched by $14$ or $16$, after which the remaining edges are forced, giving $N_1$ or $M_2$. If it uses $05$, the same two choices at vertex $1$ force $M_3$ or $N_2$.

The cuts $$\delta(T)=\{03,14,27\},\qquad
 \delta(U)=\{05,16,27\}$$ both have total marginal one at $x_e=1/3$. The matching $N_1$ crosses the first cut three times, and $N_2$ crosses the second cut three times. They therefore have probability zero in every law with those marginals. The remaining three matchings partition $E$, so their probabilities are all $1/3$. In particular the relevant entropy is $\ln3$, even though the graph has five perfect matchings in total.

At this feasible point of the full polytope, $$H(x)=\ln3,\qquad
 F(x)-B(x)=4\ln(4/3)>\ln3,$$ because $(4/3)^4=256/81>3$. The difference $F(x)-B(x)-H(x)$ is $\ln(256/243)>0$. This gives a simple eight-vertex counterexample to the coefficient-one lower bound.

<a id="cons:weighted-section"></a>
### Weighted partition functions

<span id="d:sec-weighted-interface" label="d:sec-weighted-interface"></span>

Let $G$ be a loopless labelled multigraph on $n=2m\ge2$ vertices with at least one perfect matching. Write $P=P(G)$. We pass from natural-log entropy to binary-log weights, so that the resulting estimate can be used by the rational counting algorithm.

Put $$h_2(x)=\frac{F(x)}{\ln2}=\sum_{e\in E}-x_e\log_2x_e,
 \qquad 0\log_2 0=0.$$ For $\beta\in\mathbb R^E$, define <a id="cons:weighted-definitions"></a>$$\label{cons:weighted-definitions}
 Z_\beta=\sum_{M\in\mathcal M(G)}2^{\beta\cdot\mathbf1_M},
 \qquad
 q^*=\max_{x\in P}
        [h_2(x)+\beta\cdot x].$$ The maximum exists by continuity on the compact matching polytope.

<a id="stmt-cons-weighted"></a>
<div id="cons:weighted" class="corollary">

<a id="cons:weighted-bracket"></a>
**Corollary 19** (Weighted counting estimate). *For every finite real vector $\beta$, $$\label{cons:weighted-bracket}
                q^*-\frac{2m-2}{\ln2}
                \le\log_2 Z_\beta\le q^*.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cons-weighted"><summary>Proof of stmt-cons-weighted</summary>

*Proof.* Let $p_\beta(M)=2^{\beta\cdot\mathbf1_M}/Z_\beta$, a positive probability law on the matching family. The finite entropy comparison with this law gives, for any matching law $p$ of mean $x$, $$\frac{-\sum_Mp(M)\ln p(M)}{\ln2}+\beta\cdot x
                          \le\log_2 Z_\beta,$$ with equality for $p=p_\beta$. Indeed, this is the nonnegativity of $\sum_{M:p(M)>0}p(M)\ln(p(M)/p_\beta(M))$, which follows from $\ln t\le t-1$. Taking a maximizing law at each mean and then the maximum over means therefore yields <a id="cons:variational"></a>$$\label{cons:variational}
 \log_2 Z_\beta
       =\max_{x\in P}\left[\frac{H(x)}{\ln2}+\beta\cdot x\right].$$ Corollary <a href="#cons:defect" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cons:defect">12</a> says $F(x)-(2m-2)\le H(x)\le F(x)$. Divide by $\ln2$, add $\beta\cdot x$, and maximize to obtain Equation <a href="#cons:weighted-bracket" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cons:weighted-bracket">[cons:weighted-bracket]</a>. ◻

</details>

The analytical comparison does not itself supply a numerical optimizer. The exact-arithmetic construction in Sections <a href="#d:sec-objective" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:sec-objective">7</a>–<a href="#d:sec-algorithm" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:sec-algorithm">10</a> uses the following coarser bracket, with fixed constants that leave room for its later rounding and optimization errors.

<a id="stmt-d-coarse-weighted"></a>
<div id="d:coarse-weighted" class="corollary">

<a id="d:eq-coarse-weighted"></a>
**Corollary 20** (Conservative weighted bracket). *For every finite loopless labelled multigraph on an even number $n\ge2$ of vertices with at least one perfect matching, and every finite real vector $\beta\in\mathbb R^E$, $$\label{d:eq-coarse-weighted}
 q^*-4n\le\log_2 Z_\beta\le q^*+n.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-d-coarse-weighted"><summary>Proof of stmt-d-coarse-weighted</summary>

*Proof.* With $n=2m$, Corollary <a href="#cons:weighted" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cons:weighted">19</a> gives $$q^*-\frac{n-2}{\ln2}\le\log_2Z_\beta\le q^*.$$ Since $\ln2=\int_1^2t^{-1}\,dt\ge1/2$, we have $(n-2)/\ln2\le2n-4\le4n$. Enlarging both sides of this bracket proves the claim. ◻

</details>

For the empty graph, the matching family consists of the empty matching, $Z_\beta=1$ and $q^*=0$. The counting algorithm handles that input directly. On positive even order it uses exactly Equation <a href="#d:eq-coarse-weighted" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-coarse-weighted">[d:eq-coarse-weighted]</a>; all constants in the finite optimization and final estimate below refer to this bracket.

<a id="d:sec-objective"></a>
### A rational objective for counting matchings

The weighted entropy estimate reduces counting to a continuous maximum. We now replace that maximum by one computed from exact rational data. Assume throughout this section that $n\ge4$ is even. Let $N$ be the number of perfect matchings in the input graph, with parallel edges counted as distinct choices. We allow the multiplicity $k_e$ of each unordered pair $e\in\binom V2$ to be supplied in binary; an absent pair has $k_e=0$. For a simple graph, every multiplicity is zero or one.

We work on the complete simple graph $K_n$, writing $$P=P(K_n),\qquad p=\binom n2.$$ The objective functions below have one coordinate for each of these $p$ unordered pairs. In particular, $P$ is nonempty even when the input graph has no perfect matching. Small positive weights on absent pairs will encode the input support in the objective. Completion also provides the uniform feasible mean $u_{ij}=1/(n-1)$, whose nontrivial odd-cut inequalities are strict. Section <a href="#d:sec-optimization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:sec-optimization">8</a> uses that slack to repair cut violations after correcting the degree sums.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Completing the graph with small weights

<a id="d:eq-completion-weights"></a>
Set $$\label{d:eq-completion-weights}
\begin{split}
 b_{\max}&=\max\bigl(\{0\}\cup
       \{\lfloor\log_2 k_e\rfloor:k_e>0\}\bigr),\\
 L&=100(n+b_{\max}+1)^3,\qquad
 \beta_e=
 \begin{cases}
   \lfloor\log_2 k_e\rfloor,&k_e>0,\\
   -L,&k_e=0.
 \end{cases}
\end{split}$$ For a positive integer $k_e$, its binary bit length minus one equals $\lfloor\log_2 k_e\rfloor$. Thus these coefficients are computed exactly by integer arithmetic, and their numerical magnitudes are polynomial in $n+b_{\max}$.

Use $Z_\beta$ and $q^*$ from Corollary <a href="#d:coarse-weighted" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:coarse-weighted">20</a> for $K_n$ with the weights in Equation <a href="#d:eq-completion-weights" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-completion-weights">[d:eq-completion-weights]</a>. A term in $Z_\beta$ is called valid if its matching uses only pairs with positive input multiplicity; write $Z_{\mathrm{good}}$ and $Z_{\mathrm{bad}}$ for the sums of valid and remaining terms, respectively.

<a id="stmt-d-lem-penalized-count"></a>
<div id="d:lem-penalized-count" class="lemma">

<a id="d:eq-penalized-count"></a>
**Lemma 21** (Weighted completion). *The contribution of matchings using an absent pair satisfies $$0\le Z_{\mathrm{bad}}\le2^{-20n}.$$ Moreover, $$\label{d:eq-penalized-count}
\begin{aligned}
 N=0&\quad\Longrightarrow\quad \log_2 Z_\beta\le-20n,\\
 N>0&\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
       \max\{1,2^{-n/2}N\}\le Z_\beta\le2N.
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-d-lem-penalized-count"><summary>Proof of stmt-d-lem-penalized-count</summary>

<a id="d:eq-valid-rounded-count"></a>
*Proof.* For each pair with $k_e>0$, put $r_e=2^{\lfloor\log_2 k_e\rfloor}$. Then $$\frac{k_e}{2}\le r_e\le k_e.$$ For a valid matching of $K_n$, the product of its $k_e$’s is the number of corresponding perfect matchings in the input multigraph. Replacing these $n/2$ factors by $r_e$ and summing therefore gives $$\label{d:eq-valid-rounded-count}
  2^{-n/2}N\le Z_{\mathrm{good}}\le N.$$ If $N>0$, there is at least one valid matching, and its rounded weight is a positive integer. Hence $Z_{\mathrm{good}}\ge1$.

Every remaining matching uses a pair of weight $2^{-L}$, and each of its other pair weights is at most $2^{b_{\max}}$. There are at most $2^p$ matchings, since each is a subset of the $p$ pairs. Thus $$Z_{\mathrm{bad}}\le 2^{p+b_{\max}n/2-L}\le2^{-20n}.$$ For the last inequality, put $t=n+b_{\max}+1\ge5$ and note that $$p+\frac{b_{\max}n}{2}+20n\le t^2+20t\le100t^3=L.$$ When $N=0$, every term is in $Z_{\mathrm{bad}}$; also $Z_\beta>0$ because $K_n$ has a perfect matching and every assigned weight is positive. This proves the first implication in Equation <a href="#d:eq-penalized-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-penalized-count">[d:eq-penalized-count]</a>. When $N>0$, Equation <a href="#d:eq-valid-rounded-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-valid-rounded-count">[d:eq-valid-rounded-count]</a> gives the lower bounds, while $$Z_\beta\le N+2^{-20n}\le2N$$ because $N$ is a positive integer. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Interpolating entropy on a dyadic grid

<a id="d:eq-dyadic-chords"></a>
Let $K$ be the least nonnegative integer such that $2^K\ge p$. Define $\phi(t)=-t\log_2 t$ on $[0,1]$, with $\phi(0)=0$, and let $\psi$ be its continuous linear interpolant at $$0,\ 2^{-K},\ 2^{-(K-1)},\ \ldots,\ 2^{-1},\ 1.$$ The interpolant has the explicit rational formulas $$\label{d:eq-dyadic-chords}
 \psi(t)=
 \begin{cases}
   Kt,&0\le t\le2^{-K},\\
   (k-1)t+2^{-k},
     &2^{-(k+1)}\le t\le2^{-k},\quad 0\le k<K.
 \end{cases}$$ The formulas agree at common endpoints.

<a id="stmt-d-lem-dyadic-entropy"></a>
<div id="d:lem-dyadic-entropy" class="lemma">

<a id="d:eq-dyadic-entropy-error"></a>
**Lemma 22** (Dyadic entropy approximation). *The function $\psi$ is concave, with integer slopes of absolute value at most $K$. For every $x\in P$, $$\label{d:eq-dyadic-entropy-error}
  0\le h_2(x)-\sum_e\psi(x_e)
     \le\frac n2+1\le n.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-d-lem-dyadic-entropy"><summary>Proof of stmt-d-lem-dyadic-entropy</summary>

*Proof.* At a positive knot, $\phi(2^{-k})=k2^{-k}$. The chord between $2^{-(k+1)}$ and $2^{-k}$ therefore has slope $k-1$ and intercept $2^{-k}$, whereas the chord from zero has slope $K$. This proves Equation <a href="#d:eq-dyadic-chords" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-dyadic-chords">[d:eq-dyadic-chords]</a>. Reading the intervals from left to right, the slopes are nonincreasing, so $\psi$ is concave. Since $p\ge6$, we have $K\ge1$, and all its slopes lie between $-1$ and $K$.

The concavity of $\phi$ puts every chord below its graph. If $2^{-(k+1)}\le t\le2^{-k}$, then $$\psi(t)-kt=2^{-k}-t\ge0,
  \qquad \phi(t)\le(k+1)t.$$ Consequently $0\le\phi(t)-\psi(t)\le t$ on this interval. On the interval adjacent to zero, write $t=2^{-K}r$, where $0\le r\le1$. Then $$\phi(t)-\psi(t)=2^{-K}(-r\log_2 r)\le2^{-K}.$$ Here the expression at $r=0$ is interpreted by continuity; on $(0,1]$, differentiation gives the maximum $1/(e\ln2)<1$.

For $x\in P$, summing the degree equations gives $\sum_e x_e=n/2$. Apply the bound $x_e$ on intervals above $2^{-K}$, and the bound $2^{-K}$ on the interval below it. The total error is at most $$\sum_{e:x_e\ge2^{-K}}x_e
     +\bigl|\{e:x_e<2^{-K}\}\bigr|\,2^{-K}
  \le \frac n2+p2^{-K}
  \le \frac n2+1
  \le n.$$ This proves Equation <a href="#d:eq-dyadic-entropy-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-dyadic-entropy-error">[d:eq-dyadic-entropy-error]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="d:eq-surrogate-objective"></a>
Define the concave function $f$ on the entire box $[0,1]^p$ by $$\label{d:eq-surrogate-objective}
  f(x)=\sum_e\psi(x_e)+\beta\cdot x,\qquad
  f^*=\max_{x\in P}f(x).$$ Equation <a href="#d:eq-dyadic-entropy-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-dyadic-entropy-error">[d:eq-dyadic-entropy-error]</a> gives $f(x)\le h_2(x)+\beta\cdot x\le f(x)+n$ on $P$. Taking maxima yields <a id="d:eq-surrogate-gap"></a>$$\label{d:eq-surrogate-gap}
  f^*\le q^*\le f^*+n.$$ On each affine piece, $f$ has integer coordinate slopes of absolute value at most $K+L$, since $|\beta_e|\le L$. All breakpoints and intercepts in Equation <a href="#d:eq-dyadic-chords" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-dyadic-chords">[d:eq-dyadic-chords]</a> have denominators dividing $2^K<2p$. Thus $f$ can be evaluated exactly at rational points using rational arithmetic. Section <a href="#d:sec-optimization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:sec-optimization">8</a> uses these properties to estimate $f^*$.

<a id="d:sec-optimization"></a>
### Optimization by an exact penalty

<a id="d:eq-max-violation"></a>
Retain the notation of Section <a href="#d:sec-objective" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:sec-objective">7</a>: $n\ge4$ is even, $P=P(K_n)$, $p=\binom n2$, and $f^*=\max_{x\in P}f(x)$. We first replace optimization over $P$ by optimization over the box $[0,1]^p$. For $x$ in this box, define $$\label{d:eq-max-violation}
w(x)=
\max\left\{
0,\ 
\max_{i\in V}\bigl|x(\delta(i))-1\bigr|,\ 
\max_{\substack{S\subseteq V\\ |S|\ \text{odd}}}
       \bigl(1-x(\delta(S))\bigr)
\right\}.$$ By Theorem <a href="#geom:thm-polytope" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:thm-polytope">4</a>, $w(x)=0$ exactly when $x\in P$.

<a id="stmt-d-lem-feasibility-repair"></a>
<div id="d:lem-feasibility-repair" class="lemma">

**Lemma 23** (Feasibility repair). *Set $A_0=10n^2$. For every $x\in[0,1]^p$, there is $y\in P$ such that $$\|y-x\|_\infty\le (2+A_0)w(x).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-d-lem-feasibility-repair"><summary>Proof of stmt-d-lem-feasibility-repair</summary>

<a id="d:eq-corrected-cut-bound"></a>
*Proof.* Write $w=w(x)$, $d_i=1-x(\delta(i))$, and $d_{\mathrm{tot}}=\sum_{i\in V}d_i$. Correct the degrees by setting <a id="d:eq-degree-correction"></a>$$\label{d:eq-degree-correction}
x'_{ij}
=x_{ij}+\frac{d_i+d_j}{n-2}
          -\frac{d_{\mathrm{tot}}}{(n-2)(n-1)}
\qquad (i\ne j).$$ The correction sums to $d_i$ at vertex $i$, since $$\sum_{j\ne i}(d_i+d_j)=(n-2)d_i+d_{\mathrm{tot}}.$$ Thus $x'(\delta(i))=1$ for every $i$. Since $|d_i|\le w$ and $|d_{\mathrm{tot}}|\le nw$, $$\|x'-x\|_\infty
\le
\left(\frac{2}{n-2}+\frac{n}{(n-2)(n-1)}\right)w
\le 2w.$$ Consequently $x'_e\ge-2w$, and every odd set $S$ satisfies $$\label{d:eq-corrected-cut-bound}
x'(\delta(S))\ge 1-(1+2p)w.$$

Let $u_{ij}=1/(n-1)$. This vector has degree sums one, and $$u(\delta(S))=\frac{|S|(n-|S|)}{n-1}.$$ Cuts with $|S|=1$ or $|S|=n-1$ have value one. For every nontrivial odd cut, $3\le |S|\le n-3$, and hence, when $n\ge6$, $$u(\delta(S))\ge\frac{3(n-3)}{n-1}\ge\frac32.$$ There are no nontrivial odd cuts when $n=4$. In particular, $u\in P$.

Set <a id="d:eq-uniform-repair"></a>$$\label{d:eq-uniform-repair}
t=\min\{1,A_0w\},
\qquad
y=(1-t)x'+tu.$$ If $A_0w\ge1$, including equality, then $y=u\in P$. Otherwise $t=A_0w<1$. The inequalities $$\frac{A_0}{n-1}\ge2,
\qquad
\frac{A_0}{2}\ge1+2p$$ give $$y_e\ge-2(1-t)w+\frac{A_0w}{n-1}\ge0.$$ Equation <a href="#d:eq-corrected-cut-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-corrected-cut-bound">[d:eq-corrected-cut-bound]</a> also gives, for each nontrivial odd cut, $$y(\delta(S))
\ge 1-(1-t)(1+2p)w+\frac{A_0w}{2}
\ge1.$$ The degree sums remain one, so the trivial odd cuts also have value one. Theorem <a href="#geom:thm-polytope" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:thm-polytope">4</a> now yields $y\in P$ in this branch as well.

For either branch, use the identity $$y-x=(1-t)(x'-x)+t(u-x).$$ Both $u$ and $x$ lie in the box, so $$\|y-x\|_\infty
\le 2(1-t)w+t\|u-x\|_\infty
\le 2w+t
\le (2+A_0)w.$$ This also covers $w=0$. ◻

</details>

The repair bound converts constraint violation into a quantitative distance from feasibility. We can therefore penalize violations by a fixed amount large enough to recover exactly the constrained optimum.

<a id="d:eq-penalty-parameters"></a>
Define the positive integers $M_0,D_0$ and the function $\Phi$ by $$\label{d:eq-penalty-parameters}
M_0=p(K+L)(2+A_0),\qquad
\Phi(x)=f(x)-M_0w(x),\qquad
D_0=p(K+L+M_0)^2.$$ A vector $g$ is a *supergradient* of $\Phi$ at $x$ on the box if $$\Phi(z)\le \Phi(x)+g\cdot(z-x)
\qquad\text{for every }z\in[0,1]^p.$$

<a id="stmt-d-lem-exact-penalty"></a>
<div id="d:lem-exact-penalty" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24** (Exact penalty and integral supergradients). *The function $\Phi$ is concave on $[0,1]^p$, and $$\max_{x\in[0,1]^p}\Phi(x)=f^*.$$ At every point of the box, $\Phi$ has an integral supergradient $g$ satisfying $\|g\|_2^2\le D_0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-d-lem-exact-penalty"><summary>Proof of stmt-d-lem-exact-penalty</summary>

<a id="d:eq-objective-lipschitz"></a>
*Proof.* By Lemma <a href="#d:lem-dyadic-entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:lem-dyadic-entropy">22</a>, the slopes of $\psi$ are integers of absolute value at most $K$. Since $|\beta_e|\le L$, it follows that $$\label{d:eq-objective-lipschitz}
|f(x)-f(y)|\le (K+L)\sum_e|x_e-y_e|
            \le p(K+L)\|x-y\|_\infty
\qquad (x,y\in[0,1]^p).$$ For the point $y\in P$ supplied by Lemma <a href="#d:lem-feasibility-repair" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:lem-feasibility-repair">23</a>, Equation <a href="#d:eq-objective-lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-objective-lipschitz">[d:eq-objective-lipschitz]</a> gives $$\Phi(x)
\le f(y)+p(K+L)\|x-y\|_\infty-M_0w(x)
\le f(y)\le f^*.$$ Conversely, $\Phi=f$ on $P$, proving equality of the maxima. The function $w$ is a maximum of affine functions and is therefore convex. Since $f$ is concave, $\Phi$ is concave.

Choose a supporting piece slope $\sigma_e$ of $\psi$ at each $x_e$; at a knot either adjacent slope is allowed. With $b_e=\sigma_e+\beta_e$, these choices give $$f(z)\le f(x)+b\cdot(z-x)
\qquad (z\in[0,1]^p).$$ Write each absolute value in Equation <a href="#d:eq-max-violation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-max-violation">[d:eq-max-violation]</a> as the maximum of its two affine terms. Choose any active affine term $a\cdot z+c$ in this maximum, including the zero term when it is active. Then $a_e\in\{-1,0,1\}$, and $$w(z)\ge a\cdot z+c=w(x)+a\cdot(z-x).$$ Subtracting the last inequality, multiplied by $M_0$, proves that $g=b-M_0a$ is a supergradient of $\Phi$ on the entire box. The independent choices of supporting pieces and active rows are valid also at endpoints and ties. Finally, $g$ is integral and $$|g_e|\le K+L+M_0,
\qquad
\|g\|_2^2\le p(K+L+M_0)^2=D_0.$$ ◻

</details>

We use the classical projected subgradient method (Polyak 1967, Equation (1)) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[Polyak1967](#ref-Polyak1967)] to approximate the maximum of $\Phi$. Its bounded integral supergradients permit a fixed step size and keep every iterate on one rational grid. The next lemma gives the finite iteration count and value guarantee directly.

<a id="stmt-d-lem-projected-ascent"></a>
<div id="d:lem-projected-ascent" class="lemma">

<a id="d:eq-projected-iterations"></a>
<a id="d:eq-optimization-accuracy"></a>
**Lemma 25** (Finite projected ascent). *Let $N_{\mathrm{it}}=pD_0$, set $x^{(0)}=0$, and for $j=0,\ldots,N_{\mathrm{it}}-1$ choose a supergradient $g^{(j)}$ as in Lemma <a href="#d:lem-exact-penalty" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:lem-exact-penalty">24</a> and set $$\label{d:eq-projected-iterations}
x^{(j+1)}
=\Pi_{[0,1]^p}\left(x^{(j)}+\frac{g^{(j)}}{D_0}\right),$$ where $\Pi_{[0,1]^p}$ clips each coordinate to $[0,1]$. Then $$\label{d:eq-optimization-accuracy}
R=\left\lfloor\max_{0\le j<N_{\mathrm{it}}}\Phi(x^{(j)})\right\rfloor
\qquad\text{satisfies}\qquad
R\le f^*\le R+2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-d-lem-projected-ascent"><summary>Proof of stmt-d-lem-projected-ascent</summary>

<a id="d:eq-ascent-step"></a>
*Proof.* Choose $z\in P$ with $f(z)=f^*$; then $\Phi(z)=f^*$ by Lemma <a href="#d:lem-exact-penalty" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:lem-exact-penalty">24</a>. Clipping cannot increase distance to $z\in[0,1]^p$. Expanding the squared distance and using the supergradient inequality therefore gives $$\begin{aligned}
\|x^{(j+1)}-z\|_2^2
&\le \|x^{(j)}-z\|_2^2
 +\frac{2}{D_0}g^{(j)}\cdot(x^{(j)}-z)
 +\frac{\|g^{(j)}\|_2^2}{D_0^2}\notag\\
&\le \|x^{(j)}-z\|_2^2
 -\frac{2}{D_0}\bigl(f^*-\Phi(x^{(j)})\bigr)+\frac1{D_0}.
\label{d:eq-ascent-step}
\end{aligned}$$ Summing Equation <a href="#d:eq-ascent-step" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-ascent-step">[d:eq-ascent-step]</a> over exactly the indicated $N_{\mathrm{it}}$ indices yields $$\begin{aligned}
2\sum_{j=0}^{N_{\mathrm{it}}-1}
  \bigl(f^*-\Phi(x^{(j)})\bigr)
&\le D_0\bigl(\|x^{(0)}-z\|_2^2
             -\|x^{(N_{\mathrm{it}})}-z\|_2^2\bigr)
       +N_{\mathrm{it}}\\
&\le pD_0+N_{\mathrm{it}}=2N_{\mathrm{it}}.
\end{aligned}$$ Here $\|x^{(0)}-z\|_2^2\le p$. Thus the largest of the sampled values, say $V$, satisfies $f^*-1\le V\le f^*$. Since $R=\lfloor V\rfloor$, $$R\le V\le f^*,
\qquad
f^*\le V+1<R+2,$$ which proves Equation <a href="#d:eq-optimization-accuracy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-optimization-accuracy">[d:eq-optimization-accuracy]</a>. ◻

</details>

Every iterate in Equation <a href="#d:eq-projected-iterations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-projected-iterations">[d:eq-projected-iterations]</a> has coordinates that are integer multiples of $1/D_0$: the update adds an integral vector divided by $D_0$, and clipping inserts only $0$ or $1$. To evaluate $w$ and choose an active row, compare the zero term and the $2n$ signed degree terms with the largest odd-cut violation. Lemma <a href="#d:lem-odd-cut-oracle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:lem-odd-cut-oracle">27</a> computes a minimum odd cut from the nonnegative integer capacities $D_0x_e^{(j)}$ and returns its row in the original edge coordinates. The values of $\Phi$ and the floor defining $R$ can then be computed exactly using rational arithmetic. Proposition <a href="#d:prop-bit-complexity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:prop-bit-complexity">28</a> gives the resulting bit-complexity bound.

<a id="d:sec-odd-cuts"></a>
### Computing an active odd-cut constraint

The optimization in Section <a href="#d:sec-optimization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:sec-optimization">8</a> needs both the value of $w(x)$ and an affine function attaining its defining maximum. The degree violations are immediate to compute. We supply the remaining ingredient: an exact minimum odd cut, together with its vertex set. The use of minimum odd cuts for matching-constraint separation goes back to Padberg and Rao (Padberg and Rao 1982) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[PadbergRao1982](#ref-PadbergRao1982)]. We prove the recursion and its arithmetic bounds here.

Throughout this section, an auxiliary graph has vertex set $W$ and nonnegative integer capacities $c_{ij}=c_{ji}$ for distinct $i,j\in W$. Absent edges have capacity zero. Parallel capacities are summed and loops are discarded. Write $$c(S)=\sum_{\substack{i\in S\\ j\in W\setminus S}}c_{ij},
 \qquad
 C=\sum_{\{i,j\}\subseteq W}c_{ij},
 \qquad v=|W|.$$

We use the classical augmenting-path method of Ford and Fulkerson (Ford and Fulkerson 1957, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[FordFulkerson1957](#ref-FordFulkerson1957)], specialized here to unit augmentations. The capacity-dependent bound needed below is proved explicitly.

<a id="stmt-d-lem-unit-mincut"></a>
<div id="d:lem-unit-mincut" class="lemma">

**Lemma 26** (An integral two-terminal minimum cut). *For distinct $a,b\in W$, a minimum cut separating $a$ from $b$ can be found deterministically using at most $C$ unit augmentations and $O(v^2(C+1))$ arithmetic operations on integers of $O(\log_2(v+C+2))$ bits.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-d-lem-unit-mincut"><summary>Proof of stmt-d-lem-unit-mincut</summary>

*Proof.* Maintain integers $b_{ij}=-b_{ji}$ such that $b_{ij}\le c_{ij}$ in both directions and $\sum_j b_{ij}=0$ for $i\notin\{a,b\}$. The two directional inequalities give $-c_{ij}\le b_{ij}\le c_{ij}$. Start with zero flow. If a simple directed path from $a$ to $b$ uses only steps with residual capacity $c_{ij}-b_{ij}>0$, increase the flow on each forward step by one and decrease its reverse by one. Integrality makes this feasible, conservation is preserved, and the net source outflow $q=\sum_j b_{aj}$ increases by one. Since $$q\le \sum_j c_{aj}\le C,$$ there are at most $C$ augmentations.

When no such path exists, let $S$ be the vertices reachable from $a$ by positive-residual steps. Then $b\notin S$, and every step from $S$ to its complement is saturated. Antisymmetry cancels internal flows, so conservation gives $$q=\sum_{\substack{i\in S\\j\notin S}}b_{ij}
   =\sum_{\substack{i\in S\\j\notin S}}c_{ij}=c(S).$$ For every other $a$–$b$ cut, the same conservation identity and $b_{ij}\le c_{ij}$ give capacity at least $q$. Thus $S$ is minimum. A graph search with fixed vertex order finds a simple residual path, or the final reachable set, in $O(v^2)$ operations. There are at most $C+1$ searches, and all flows and residual capacities have magnitude at most $2C$. The argument includes $C=0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-d-lem-odd-cut-oracle"></a>
<div id="d:lem-odd-cut-oracle" class="lemma">

**Lemma 27** (Minimum odd-terminal cut). *Let $U\subseteq W$ have positive even cardinality. One can deterministically find a set $S\subseteq W$ minimizing $c(S)$ subject to $|S\cap U|$ being odd. The algorithm returns the set itself, uses at most $|U|-1$ recursive instances, and requires $O(|U|^3v^2(C+1))$ arithmetic operations on integers of $O(\log_2(v+C+2))$ bits.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-d-lem-odd-cut-oracle"><summary>Proof of stmt-d-lem-odd-cut-oracle</summary>

*Proof.* Call a cut *terminal-separating* if both sides meet $U$. Compute a minimum cut for every pair of distinct terminals using Lemma <a href="#d:lem-unit-mincut" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:lem-unit-mincut">26</a>, and take the least-capacity result, with sides $A$ and $\overline A=W\setminus A$. This is a minimum terminal-separating cut: every such cut separates some terminal pair, and every recorded pair cut is terminal-separating.

If $|A\cap U|$ is odd, return $A$. Every admissible odd-terminal cut is terminal-separating, so this answer is optimal. Otherwise both $A\cap U$ and $\overline A\cap U$ have positive even cardinality. Form two children:

1.  keep $A$, contract $\overline A$ to a new nonterminal, and use terminal set $A\cap U$;

2.  keep $\overline A$, contract $A$ to a new nonterminal, and use terminal set $\overline A\cap U$.

For example, in the first child the capacity from $i\in A$ to the new vertex is $\sum_{j\in\overline A}c_{ij}$. Recurse on both children and return the better lifted cut. Every call strictly decreases the positive even terminal count; when $|U|=2$, the first cut is already odd.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
###### Lifting cuts.

A child cut lifts by replacing its contracted vertex, if included, with the entire block it represents. The crossing capacities are unchanged, and including that block adds an even number of parent terminals. Thus the lift has the same capacity and is terminal-odd.

This remains true through arbitrarily nested contractions. Relative to the initial instance, current vertices form a partition into blocks. Every current terminal is an original terminal singleton, and every nonterminal block contains an even number of original terminals. Initially the latter count is zero; each contraction combines an even number of current terminals with blocks of even parity, preserving the assertion. Capacities between current vertices are sums of original capacities between their blocks. Consequently every returned subset lifts to an original subset with the same capacity and terminal parity. Figure <a href="#d:fig-odd-cut-lift" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:fig-odd-cut-lift">2</a> illustrates the parity rule.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
###### Preserving an optimum.

Let $S$ be an optimal odd-terminal side in the current instance. Exactly one of $S\cap A$ and $S\cap\overline A$ contains an odd number of terminals; exchange the names of the two sides so that it is $S\cap A$. Because $|A\cap U|$ is even, replacing $S$ by its complement preserves this odd intersection. Make this replacement if $S$ contains all terminals of $\overline A$. Now $S\cup A$ contains terminals of $A$ and omits a terminal of $\overline A$, so $$c(S\cup A)\ge c(A).$$ For any two vertex sets, nonnegative undirected capacities satisfy <a id="d:eq-cut-submodularity"></a>$$\label{d:eq-cut-submodularity}
 c(S\cap A)+c(S\cup A)\le c(S)+c(A).$$ Indeed, the right side minus the left side is twice the capacity between $S\setminus A$ and $A\setminus S$. It follows that $c(S\cap A)\le c(S)$. The set $S\cap A$ is a feasible odd-terminal side in the first child, avoiding its contracted vertex. Hence at least one child has optimum at most the parent’s. Conversely, lifting shows that every child optimum is at least the parent’s. Induction on $|U|$ proves that the better recursive answer attains the parent optimum.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
###### Size and running time.

Each nonleaf splits its terminal set into two disjoint nonempty even sets. There are at most $|U|/2$ leaves and hence at most $|U|-1$ instances in this binary recursion tree. Each contracted side contains at least two terminals, so a child also has fewer vertices than its parent. Within any one instance, every surviving capacity sums a disjoint set of original edge capacities; original edges internal to blocks are discarded. Its total capacity is therefore at most $C$. Capacities in different children may overlap, and we account for them by multiplying the cost of an instance by the number of instances.

There are at most $O(|U|^2)$ pair computations per instance, each costing $O(v^2(C+1))$ by Lemma <a href="#d:lem-unit-mincut" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:lem-unit-mincut">26</a>. Contractions and lifted-set bookkeeping fit within the same bound. This proves the stated running time. All comparisons and tie choices may use fixed orderings; zero capacities require no perturbation. ◻

</details>

<figure id="d:fig-odd-cut-lift">

<figcaption>Lifting through an even-terminal contraction. Filled dots are terminals and the open dot represents the contracted block. In this schematic instance each side initially has two terminals. The shaded child side lifts to the shaded parent side by taking the entire contracted block, preserving odd parity. Parallel crossing capacities are added, so the cut capacity is preserved as well.</figcaption>
</figure>
