<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
###### Recovering an active row in the original coordinates.

At an optimization trial point, $D_0x_e$ is a nonnegative integer for every original complete-graph pair $e$. Indeed, the initial point is zero, and an integral supergradient step of size $1/D_0$, followed by clipping to $[0,1]$, preserves this grid. Apply Lemma <a href="#d:lem-odd-cut-oracle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:lem-odd-cut-oracle">27</a> with $U=V$ and capacities $c_e=D_0x_e$. Store each contracted vertex’s block of original vertices, so the returned cut yields an explicit original set $S\subseteq V$ of odd cardinality. It minimizes $x(\delta(S))$.

The corresponding affine function in the definition of $w$ is $$\ell_S(y)=1-\sum_{e\in\delta(S)}y_e.$$ Its coefficient is $-1$ on *every* original pair crossing $S$, including pairs with $x_e=0$, and zero elsewhere. Thus the row is recovered by scanning the original pairs, even if zero-capacity edges were omitted during graph searches. Edges discarded as contraction loops have both endpoints in one block and do not cross a lifted cut. Comparing $\ell_S(x)$ with zero and the two signed degree violations for every vertex gives $w(x)$ and an affine row attaining it, as required in Section <a href="#d:sec-optimization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:sec-optimization">8</a>.

Here the initial total capacity is at most $pD_0$. A merged edge can exceed $D_0$, but the bound on each instance’s total capacity remains $pD_0$; likewise a large reverse residual does not change the source-outflow bound in Lemma <a href="#d:lem-unit-mincut" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:lem-unit-mincut">26</a>. One complete violation computation therefore takes $$O\!\left(n^5(pD_0+1)\right)$$ arithmetic operations. The numerical bounds on $D_0$ and the resulting bit complexity are recorded in Proposition <a href="#d:prop-bit-complexity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:prop-bit-complexity">28</a>.

<a id="d:sec-algorithm"></a>
### The algorithm and its guarantee

We now assemble the preceding constructions into a deterministic algorithm. The input consists of a vertex set $V=\{1,\ldots,n\}$ and nonnegative integer multiplicities $k_{\{i,j\}}$ for distinct vertex pairs, written in binary; an omitted pair has multiplicity zero. Listing parallel edges individually is also allowed, since their multiplicities can first be counted. Let $N$ be the number of perfect matchings, with parallel edge choices distinguished. Fix the vertex and pair orderings, and use the deterministic cut procedure of Section <a href="#d:sec-odd-cuts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:sec-odd-cuts">9</a>.

1.  **Handle the elementary cases.** If $n=0$, return $1$. If $n$ is odd, return $0$. If $n=2$, return $k_{\{1,2\}}$. Sort the endpoint labels occurring in positive-multiplicity pair records and count the distinct labels. If fewer than $n$ occur, an isolated vertex forces the count to be zero; return $0$. This test uses the input records even when $n$ is supplied in binary, before allocating any array indexed by all vertices or pairs.

2.  **Set up the complete-pair objective.** Now $n\ge4$ is even. Use all $p=\binom n2$ pair coordinates. Compute $b_{\max},L,\beta$ from Equation <a href="#d:eq-completion-weights" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-completion-weights">[d:eq-completion-weights]</a>, and let $K$ be the least nonnegative integer with $2^K\ge p$. Define $\psi$ and $f$ by Equations <a href="#d:eq-dyadic-chords" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-dyadic-chords">[d:eq-dyadic-chords]</a> and <a href="#d:eq-surrogate-objective" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-surrogate-objective">[d:eq-surrogate-objective]</a>. Set $A_0=10n^2$, define $w$ by Equation <a href="#d:eq-max-violation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-max-violation">[d:eq-max-violation]</a>, and compute $M_0,\Phi,D_0$ from Equation <a href="#d:eq-penalty-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-penalty-parameters">[d:eq-penalty-parameters]</a>. Set $$N_{\mathrm{it}}=pD_0,\qquad a^{(0)}=0\in\mathbb Z^p.$$ The integer vector $a^{(j)}$ represents $x^{(j)}=a^{(j)}/D_0$.

3.  **Evaluate and update $N_{\mathrm{it}}$ trial points.** For $j=0,\ldots,N_{\mathrm{it}}-1$, do the following.

    1.  Apply Lemma <a href="#d:lem-odd-cut-oracle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:lem-odd-cut-oracle">27</a> to the integral capacities $a^{(j)}_e$, with every vertex terminal, obtaining a minimum-capacity odd cut $S_j$. At $x=x^{(j)}$, compare $$0,\quad x(\delta(1))-1,\quad 1-x(\delta(1)),\quad\ldots,\quad
         x(\delta(n))-1,\quad 1-x(\delta(n)),\quad 1-x(\delta(S_j)).$$ Their maximum is $w(x)$. Select the first maximizing expression in this list and let $r^{(j)}\in\{-1,0,1\}^p$ be its linear coefficient vector. Evaluate $\Phi(x)$ exactly and retain the largest trial value seen so far.

    2.  In each coordinate choose the right-hand slope $\sigma_e^{(j)}$ of $\psi$ at $x_e^{(j)}$, using the left-hand slope at $1$. Set $$g_e^{(j)}
                        =\sigma_e^{(j)}+\beta_e-M_0r_e^{(j)}.$$ These are the integral supergradients from Lemma <a href="#d:lem-exact-penalty" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:lem-exact-penalty">24</a>.

    3.  Update the integer numerators by $$a_e^{(j+1)}
               =\min\!\left\{D_0,\,
                     \max\{0,a_e^{(j)}+g_e^{(j)}\}\right\}
               \qquad(e\in E(K_n)).$$

4.  **Return the estimate.** Let $$R=\left\lfloor
                      \max_{0\le j<N_{\mathrm{it}}}\Phi(x^{(j)})
                  \right\rfloor.$$ If $R<-10n$, return $0$. Otherwise return $$A=2^{\rho},
                 \qquad \rho=\max\{0,R-5n\}.$$

The slope convention and the ordering in Step 3 settle all remaining ties. The numerator update is exactly the clipped step $x^{(j)}+g^{(j)}/D_0$ of Lemma <a href="#d:lem-projected-ascent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:lem-projected-ascent">25</a>.

<details id="proof-stmt-d-lem-odd-cut-oracle-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-d-lem-odd-cut-oracle</summary>

*Approximation guarantee in Theorem <a href="#thm:algorithm-guarantee" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:algorithm-guarantee">2</a>.* The elementary cases return the exact count, so suppose $n\ge4$ is even and the algorithm reaches the optimization. Lemma <a href="#d:lem-projected-ascent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:lem-projected-ascent">25</a>, Lemma <a href="#d:lem-dyadic-entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:lem-dyadic-entropy">22</a>, and Corollary <a href="#d:coarse-weighted" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:coarse-weighted">20</a> give, respectively, $$R\le f^*\le R+2,\qquad
 f^*\le q^*\le f^*+n,\qquad
 q^*-4n\le\log_2 Z_\beta\le q^*+n.$$ Combining them yields <a id="d:eq-algorithm-partition-bracket"></a>$$\label{d:eq-algorithm-partition-bracket}
       R-4n\le\log_2 Z_\beta
              \le R+2n+2\le R+3n.$$ If $N=0$, Lemma <a href="#d:lem-penalized-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:lem-penalized-count">21</a> gives $\log_2 Z_\beta\le-20n$, hence $R\le-16n<-10n$. If $N>0$, the same lemma gives $Z_\beta\ge1$, hence $R\ge-3n>-10n$. Thus the zero test is exact.

<a id="d:eq-algorithm-count-bracket"></a>
Now let $N>0$. The inequalities $Z_\beta\le2N$ and $Z_\beta\ge2^{-n/2}N$, again from Lemma <a href="#d:lem-penalized-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:lem-penalized-count">21</a>, imply $$\label{d:eq-algorithm-count-bracket}
 R-5n
   \le R-4n-1
   \le\log_2 N
   \le R+\frac{7n}{2}
   \le R+4n.$$ Also $\log_2N\ge0$, so $\rho=\max\{0,R-5n\}\le\log_2N$. Since $\rho\ge R-5n$, Equation <a href="#d:eq-algorithm-count-bracket" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-algorithm-count-bracket">[d:eq-algorithm-count-bracket]</a> gives $\log_2N-\rho\le9n$. Exponentiating proves $A\le N\le2^{9n}A=512^nA$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-d-prop-bit-complexity"></a>
<div id="d:prop-bit-complexity" class="proposition">

**Proposition 28** (Bit complexity). *The algorithm above is uniform and deterministic, and its running time and output length are polynomial in the input bit length.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-d-prop-bit-complexity"><summary>Proof of stmt-d-prop-bit-complexity</summary>

<a id="d:eq-parameter-size-bounds"></a>
*Proof.* The elementary cases require only integer operations on the input. In the remaining case put $\tau=n+b_{\max}+1$, so $\tau\ge5$. The parameters have polynomial numerical magnitude, not just polynomial bit length. Indeed $p\le\tau^2$, $K\le n\le\tau$, $2^K<2p$, $L=100\tau^3$, and $A_0\le10\tau^2$. Consequently $$\label{d:eq-parameter-size-bounds}
\begin{aligned}
 M_0
   &=p(K+L)(2+A_0)
        \le1212\,\tau^7,\\
 D_0
   &=p(K+L+M_0)^2
        \le1313^2\,\tau^{16},\\
 N_{\mathrm{it}}
   &=pD_0
        \le1313^2\,\tau^{18}.
\end{aligned}$$ Here $K+L\le101\tau^3$ and $2+A_0\le12\tau^2$ give the first inequality, and $K+L+M_0\le1313\tau^7$ gives the second. The exponent of each positive multiplicity is obtained from its binary length: $\lfloor\log_2 k_e\rfloor=\operatorname{bitlen}(k_e)-1$. No logarithm is evaluated numerically.

The update preserves $a_e^{(j)}\in\{0,\ldots,D_0\}$, because every supergradient coordinate is integral. Thus every iterate has denominator dividing $D_0$. On a noninitial dyadic interval the affine formula for $\psi$ is $(k-1)t+2^{-k}$, and on the interval next to zero it is $Kt$. The intercept denominators therefore divide $2^K$. Every degree or cut sum, and hence $w(x^{(j)})$, has denominator dividing $D_0$. A single common denominator for all trial objectives is <a id="d:eq-fixed-objective-denominator"></a>$$\label{d:eq-fixed-objective-denominator}
                             Q=D_0\,2^K.$$ This denominator does not grow with the number of iterations or with changes of dyadic piece. The piece comparisons, objective comparisons, and the final floor can all be made by exact integer arithmetic.

For an explicit bound on the operands, $0\le w(x)\le n$ on the box, and $$|f(x)|\le p(1+L),\qquad
        |\Phi(x)|\le p(1+L)+nM_0,
        \qquad x\in[0,1]^p.$$ Together with Equation <a href="#d:eq-parameter-size-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="d:eq-parameter-size-bounds">[d:eq-parameter-size-bounds]</a> and $2^K<2p$, these bounds show that all objective numerators with denominator $Q$ have polynomial magnitude in $\tau$. The supergradients, scaled capacities, and counters do as well. Their bit lengths are therefore $O(\log\tau)$.

At any trial point, the cut oracle receives capacities $a_e^{(j)}$, not the original multiplicities. Their total is at most $pD_0$. After any sequence of contractions, each surviving capacity is a sum of original capacities between two disjoint vertex blocks; internal edges are discarded. Every original edge contributes to at most one surviving pair, so the same total bound holds throughout the recursion. By Lemma <a href="#d:lem-unit-mincut" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:lem-unit-mincut">26</a>, each terminal-pair computation uses at most $pD_0$ unit augmentations. Lemma <a href="#d:lem-odd-cut-oracle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:lem-odd-cut-oracle">27</a> uses $O(n)$ recursive calls, each with $O(n^2)$ terminal-pair computations. Dense residual path searches take $O(n^2)$ elementary operations. The entire optimization thus uses at most $$O\!\left(N_{\mathrm{it}}\,n^5(pD_0+1)\right)
                         =O(\tau^{41})$$ elementary graph and arithmetic operations, on integers of $O(\log\tau)$ bits. Standard integer arithmetic gives a polynomial bit bound.

It remains to bound the length of the explicit integer output. Lemma <a href="#d:lem-projected-ascent" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:lem-projected-ascent">25</a> gives $R\le f^*$. Since $\psi(t)\le -t\log_2t\le1$ on $[0,1]$, $\beta_e\le b_{\max}$, and $\sum_e x_e=n/2$ on $P(K_n)$, we have $f^*\le p+b_{\max}n/2$. This upper bound is nonnegative, so the clamped exponent satisfies <a id="d:eq-clamped-output-size"></a>$$\label{d:eq-clamped-output-size}
 \rho=\max\{0,R-5n\}
       \le\max\{0,R\}
       \le\max\{0,f^*\}
       \le p+\frac{b_{\max}n}{2}.$$ Writing $2^\rho$ as a binary integer therefore takes $O(n^2+nb_{\max}+1)$ bits and operations. The zero and exact elementary outputs also have polynomial length.

For an explicit vertex list, both $n$ and $b_{\max}$ are bounded by the input bit length. The same conclusion holds when $n$ is given in binary and only positive pair records are listed. The coverage test in Step 1 takes polynomial time in the input bit length. If it does not return zero, every vertex appears in a record, so $n$ is at most twice the number of positive pair records. Thus the complete-pair allocation and the optimization are polynomial in this encoding as well. ◻

</details>

Theorem <a href="#thm:algorithm-guarantee" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:algorithm-guarantee">2</a> and Proposition <a href="#d:prop-bit-complexity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:prop-bit-complexity">28</a> give both the approximation guarantee and its uniform polynomial bit implementation. If an upper estimate is preferred, $512^nA$ lies between $N$ and $512^nN$.

<a id="a:section"></a>
### Alternative geometric and entropy arguments

We give two other descriptions of supported face codimension: a laminar family of tight cuts, and a quotient-space formulation of contraction. Both recover the bound used in the main proof. We then retain the direct coefficient-three and coefficient-four covariance comparisons and their counting consequences. These comparisons use weaker dimension estimates; they do not require the finite-order entropy theorem.

Throughout, $G=(V,E)$ is a finite loopless labelled multigraph on $n=2m\ge2$ vertices with a nonempty matching polytope, denoted by $P=P(G)$. We use $F_x$, $T(x)$, and $d(x)=\dim T(x)$ from Lemma <a href="#geom:lem-face" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:lem-face">5</a>. Its direction-space description and Theorem <a href="#geom:thm-polytope" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:thm-polytope">4</a> are the shared geometric preliminaries. Removing the zero coordinates of a mean preserves the dimension of its minimal face: coordinate restriction and zero extension are inverse affine maps on that face. We use this identification whenever we work only on supported edges.

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### Uncrossing and a laminar rank bound

This alternative proof uses the laminar uncrossing method for tight odd cuts, as in (Svensson and Tarnawski 2017, full version, Lemma 2.2 and Appendix A) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[SvenssonTarnawski2017](#ref-SvenssonTarnawski2017)]; compare also the tight-cut rank estimate in (Abdi et al. 2026, Lemma 6.5) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[AbdiCornuejolsDadushDalirrooyfard2026](#ref-AbdiCornuejolsDadushDalirrooyfard2026)]. We give the spanning argument and the tree count explicitly, retaining the dependence between two complementary children of the root.

Recall that $\chi_S=\mathbf1_{\delta(S)}$ and that $\mathcal S_x$ is the family of odd sets whose cuts are tight at $x$. Equation <a href="#geom:eq-rank" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:eq-rank">[geom:eq-rank]</a> identifies the supported face codimension with the rank of these cut vectors after zero coordinates are removed. We first choose a laminar spanning family, then bound its rank by a tree count.

A family of sets is *laminar* if every two of its members are disjoint or one contains the other. Two sets *cross* if they intersect and neither contains the other.

<a id="stmt-geom-lem-laminar"></a>
<div id="geom:lem-laminar" class="lemma">

**Lemma 29** (Laminar spanning family). *Suppose $x\in P(G)$ and $x_e>0$ for every edge. There is a laminar family $\mathcal L\subseteq\mathcal S_x$ whose cut vectors are linearly independent and span all the cut vectors $\chi_S$, $S\in\mathcal S_x$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-lem-laminar"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-lem-laminar</summary>

*Proof.* First let $A,B\in\mathcal S_x$ cross, and put $$I=A\cap B,\qquad D=A\setminus B,\qquad
 D'=B\setminus A,\qquad O=V\setminus(A\cup B).$$ The first three regions are nonempty; $O$ may be empty. If $|I|$ is odd, then both $I$ and $A\cup B$ are odd, and the coordinatewise cut identity is <a id="geom:eq-uncross-intersection"></a>$$\label{geom:eq-uncross-intersection}
 \chi_A+\chi_B=
 \chi_I+\chi_{A\cup B}+2\mathbf1_{E(D,D')}.$$ Here $E(D,D')$ denotes the edges joining those two regions. Taking the dot product with $x$, the left side equals two, the first two terms on the right are at least one each, and the last term is nonnegative. Thus both new odd sets are tight and $x(E(D,D'))=0$. Since every coordinate of $x$ is positive, there are no edges in $E(D,D')$, so its indicator vector vanishes.

<a id="geom:eq-uncross-tight"></a>
If $|I|$ is even, then $D,D'$ are odd and instead <a id="geom:eq-uncross-difference"></a>$$\label{geom:eq-uncross-difference}
 \chi_A+\chi_B=
 \chi_D+\chi_{D'}+2\mathbf1_{E(I,O)}.$$ The same argument shows that $D,D'$ are tight and that the last indicator vanishes. Both identities hold edge by edge, including for parallel edges. All replacement sets are nonempty and proper: in the first case $A\cup B$ is odd and so cannot be the even set $V$, and in the second case the differences are nonempty proper subsets. In particular, if $A\cup B=V$, only the second case can occur. We have proved that in either case the two replacements $U,W$ belong to $\mathcal S_x$ and satisfy $$\label{geom:eq-uncross-tight}
 \chi_A+\chi_B=\chi_U+\chi_W.$$

Choose an inclusionwise maximal laminar subfamily $\mathcal L$ of $\mathcal S_x$ with independent cut vectors, and let $R$ be their span. Suppose a tight cut vector lies outside $R$. Among the corresponding tight sets choose $A$ crossing as few members of $\mathcal L$ as possible. It crosses some $B\in\mathcal L$, for otherwise it could be added to the family while preserving both laminarity and independence. Uncross $A,B$ as above. At least one replacement vector lies outside $R$, since otherwise Equation <a href="#geom:eq-uncross-tight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:eq-uncross-tight">[geom:eq-uncross-tight]</a> and $\chi_B\in R$ would give $\chi_A\in R$.

Each replacement is laminar with $B$. Also, neither replacement can cross a member $C\in\mathcal L$ that did not cross $A$. Indeed, such a set $C$ is laminar with both $A$ and $B$. If $C$ contains $A$, then it meets $B$, and laminarity forces it to contain $B$ as well: the other nesting direction would imply $A\subseteq B$. Thus $C$ contains $A\cup B$. If $C\subseteq A$, laminarity with $B$ puts $C$ inside $I$ or $D$; it cannot contain $B$, since $B\nsubseteq A$. Finally, if $C$ is disjoint from $A$, its relation to $B$ puts it inside $D'$ or $O$. These cases exhaust the possibilities. Every set among $I,A\cup B,D,D'$ is laminar with each set in this classification.

Consequently the replacement whose vector is outside $R$ crosses only previously crossed members of $\mathcal L$, and it no longer crosses $B$. This contradicts the minimal crossing count of $A$. Hence $\mathcal L$ spans every tight cut vector. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-geom-lem-laminar-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-geom-lem-laminar</summary>

*Laminar proof of Proposition <a href="#geom:prop-rank" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:prop-rank">6</a>.* Take the laminar spanning family from Lemma <a href="#geom:lem-laminar" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:lem-laminar">29</a>. Adjoin all singleton sets, if missing, and then adjoin $V$ as a root. The resulting inclusion tree has $2m$ singleton leaves. Each internal node is the disjoint union of its children, because all singletons are present. Every proper node has odd cardinality. A nonroot internal node therefore has an odd number of children, at least three. The root has an even number $c$ of children, at least two.

If $q$ is the number of nonroot internal nodes, the tree identity for the number of leaves gives $$2m-1
 =\sum_{S\text{ internal}}
          \bigl(\#\text{children of }S-1\bigr)
 \ge(c-1)+2q.$$ Hence the number of proper nodes is $2m+q\le3m-c/2$. If $c\ge4$, their cut vectors have rank at most $3m-2$ by this count. If $c=2$, the two root children are complementary sets, and their cut vectors coincide. The rank is then at most the number of proper nodes minus one, again at most $3m-2$. The original laminar spanning family is contained among these proper nodes. Equation <a href="#geom:eq-rank" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geom:eq-rank">[geom:eq-rank]</a> now proves the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### The quotient-space contraction argument

The main proof assembles compatible directions in the two contracted graphs. The same dimension count can instead be expressed as a map to the quotient spaces of child constraints. We include both the sharp estimate and the weaker forms used in the entropy comparisons below.

<a id="stmt-a-prop-supported-codimension"></a>
<div id="a:prop-supported-codimension" class="proposition">

**Proposition 30** (Supported face codimension). *For a loopless labelled multigraph on $n=2m\ge2$ vertices and $x\in P(G)$, put $A=\{e:x_e>0\}$ and $s_A=|A|$. Then $s_A-d(x)\le3m-2$. In particular, <a id="a:eq-full-support-codimension"></a>$$\label{a:eq-full-support-codimension}
 s_A-d(x)\le 2(n-2)\quad(n\ge4),
 \qquad s_A-d(x)=1\quad(n=2).$$ Hence $s_A-d(x)\le2n=4m$. For the empty graph, $P(G)$ is a singleton in zero coordinates and the codimension is zero.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-a-prop-supported-codimension"><summary>Proof of stmt-a-prop-supported-codimension</summary>

*Proof.* Identify $T(x)$ with a subspace $U_x$ of $\mathbb R^A$. We prove the sharp estimate by induction on the even order $n$. At order two, the supported matching polytope is a simplex, whose positive point has codimension one. For $n\ge4$, if no nontrivial odd cut is tight, only the degree equations constrain $U_x$. Thus its codimension is at most $n\le3n/2-2$.

Otherwise choose a tight nontrivial cut $\delta(S)$. Contract each side in turn as in the main geometric proof, obtaining $x_i\in P(G_i)$, supported edge sets $A_i=\{e:(x_i)_e>0\}$, and direction spaces $U_i=T(x_i)\subseteq\mathbb R^{A_i}$. The restrictions are feasible by the odd-cut check in the proof of Theorem <a href="#geom:thm-polytope" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:thm-polytope">4</a>. The orders satisfy $4\le n_i<n$ and $n_1+n_2=n+2$. Let $C=A\cap\delta(S)$; this set is nonempty since $x(C)=1$. Every label in $C$ occurs in both $A_i$.

Let $r_i:\mathbb R^A\to\mathbb R^{A_i}$ restrict coordinates, and define <a id="a:eq-quotient-map"></a>$$\label{a:eq-quotient-map}
 \mathcal Q:\mathbb R^A\longrightarrow
  (\mathbb R^{A_1}/U_1)\oplus(\mathbb R^{A_2}/U_2),\qquad
 \mathcal Q(u)=(r_1(u)+U_1,r_2(u)+U_2).$$ For $u\in\ker\mathcal Q$, both restrictions are child face directions. Choose a common positive radius such that $x_i+t r_i(u)\in P(G_i)$ in both signs of $t$. The perturbed means agree on every cut coordinate. Lemma <a href="#geom:lem-gluing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:lem-gluing">[geom:lem-gluing]</a> glues them to give $x+tu\in P(G)$, so $\ker\mathcal Q\subseteq U_x$. In particular, all tight parent cuts are preserved, including those crossing $S$: their nonnegative slacks cannot change in opposite signs under these two feasible perturbations.

There is one dependence between the two quotient requirements. The cut-sum functionals $$\ell_i:\mathbb R^{A_i}/U_i\longrightarrow\mathbb R,
 \qquad \ell_i(z+U_i)=\sum_{e\in C}z_e$$ are well-defined because every child direction preserves the degree of the contracted vertex. Each is nonzero, as the class of a unit vector on any cut label has value one. For every $u\in\mathbb R^A$, $$\ell_1(r_1(u)+U_1)=\sum_{e\in C}u_e
                 =\ell_2(r_2(u)+U_2).$$ Thus the image of $\mathcal Q$ lies in the kernel of the nonzero functional $(v_1,v_2)\mapsto\ell_1(v_1)-\ell_2(v_2)$. This kernel has codimension one in the full quotient target. Rank–nullity and induction now give $$\begin{aligned}
 s_A-d(x)&=\operatorname{codim}U_x
 \le\operatorname{codim}\ker\mathcal Q=\operatorname{rank}\mathcal Q\\
 &\le\operatorname{codim}U_1+\operatorname{codim}U_2-1\\
 &\le(3n_1/2-2)+(3n_2/2-2)-1=3n/2-2.
\end{aligned}$$ For $n\ge4$, this is at most $2(n-2)$. The order-two simplex and the empty graph give the remaining assertions. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
#### The exact fractional residual cases

For a general point $x\in P(G)$, put $$E_f(x)=\{e\in E:0<x_e<1\}.$$ Let $V_f(x)$ be the set of vertices incident to these fractional edges, and put $s_f=|V_f(x)|$. The following consequence isolates the coordinates needed for the entropy estimate.

<a id="stmt-a-cor-fractional-dimension"></a>
<div id="a:cor-fractional-dimension" class="corollary">

<a id="a:eq-fractional-dimension"></a>
**Corollary 31** (Fractional coordinates). *For every $x\in P(G)$, the integer $s_f$ is even, and $$\label{a:eq-fractional-dimension}
 d(x)\ge
 \begin{cases}
 |E_f(x)|-2(s_f-2),&s_f\ge4,\\
 |E_f(x)|-1,&s_f=2,\\
 0,&s_f=0,
 \end{cases}
 \qquad
       \sum_{e\in E_f(x)}x_e=\frac{s_f}{2}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-a-cor-fractional-dimension"><summary>Proof of stmt-a-cor-fractional-dimension</summary>

*Proof.* An edge with value one exhausts the degree equation at both its endpoints, so neither endpoint meets a fractional edge. Conversely, at a vertex outside $V_f(x)$, all incident coordinates are zero or one and sum to one. Exactly one of those edges has value one. Thus the vertices outside $V_f(x)$ are covered by disjoint fixed edges, and $s_f$ is even.

Let $H$ be the graph with vertex set $V_f(x)$ and edge set $E_f(x)$, and let $\bar x$ be the restriction of $x$ to $E_f(x)$. A matching law with mean $x$ uses every value-one edge and no value-zero edge with probability one. Deleting the fixed edges therefore gives a perfect matching law on $H$ with mean $\bar x$, whose coordinates are all positive. Conversely, adjoining the fixed edges to any perfect matching of $H$ gives a perfect matching of $G$. This affine extension of $P(H)$ into $P(G)$ sends $\bar x$ to $x$, and its linear part injects the two-sided direction space at $\bar x$ into $T(x)$.

If $s_f\ge 4$, Proposition <a href="#a:prop-supported-codimension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop-supported-codimension">30</a> now gives $$d(x)\ge \dim T(\bar x)
       \ge |E_f(x)|-2(s_f-2).$$ If $s_f=2$, the residual graph consists of $k=|E_f(x)|\ge 2$ distinguished parallel edges. Its polytope is the simplex $\{z\ge 0:\sum_e z_e=1\}$, and the positive point $\bar x$ has direction dimension $k-1$. Hence $d(x)\ge k-1=|E_f(x)|-1$. If $s_f=0$, the point is a matching vector and the dimension inequality is immediate; the minimal face is the singleton consisting of that matching vector.

Finally, each vertex of $V_f(x)$ has total fractional incident value one. Summing these degree equations counts each fractional edge twice, giving the second identity in Equation <a href="#a:eq-fractional-dimension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq-fractional-dimension">[a:eq-fractional-dimension]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
#### The coefficient-three and coefficient-four covariance proofs

The coarser dimension estimates above already imply the following entropy bounds. We use the continuity and face-law results from Lemmas <a href="#ent:continuity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ent:continuity">8</a> and <a href="#ent:exponential" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ent:exponential">9</a>, and the entropy comparison in Equation <a href="#ent:comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ent:comparison">[ent:comparison]</a>. The calculation keeps fixed coordinates in the ambient space and takes derivatives only on the fractional coordinates. It therefore displays directly how the exact residual cases distinguish coefficients three and four.

<div class="samepage">

<a id="stmt-a-thm-separable"></a>
<div id="a:thm-separable" class="theorem">

**Theorem 32** (Separable entropy bounds by covariance). *Let $G$ be a finite loopless labelled multigraph of even order $n=2m$, with a nonempty perfect-matching family. With natural logarithms and continuous endpoint conventions, define $$J_c(x)=F(x)-cB(x)
   =\sum_{e\in E}\bigl[-x_e\ln x_e+c(1-x_e)\ln(1-x_e)\bigr].$$ For each $c\in\{3,4\}$ and every $x\in P(G)$, <a id="a:eq-separable-bounds"></a>$$\label{a:eq-separable-bounds}
 J_c(x)\le H(x)\le F(x)+B(x).$$ For $n=0$, all displayed quantities are zero.*

</div>

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-a-thm-separable"><summary>Proof of stmt-a-thm-separable</summary>

*Proof.* The empty graph gives the stated trivial case. Assume $n\ge2$. For the upper bound, regard a matching law with mean $x$ as a law on $\{0,1\}^E$, giving zero probability to nonmatching vectors. Compare it with independent Bernoulli coordinates of respective means $x_e$. This product law is positive wherever the matching law is positive: a coordinate with mean zero or one is necessarily fixed at that value under both laws. Equation <a href="#ent:comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ent:comparison">[ent:comparison]</a> now gives the upper bound.

For the lower bound, fix either $c=3$ or $c=4$. Lemma <a href="#ent:continuity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ent:continuity">8</a> and continuity of $J_c$ imply that $H-J_c$ attains its minimum on the compact set $P$. At an integral mean the only possible law is the point mass at that matching vector, so $H=J_c=0$. It suffices to rule out a fractional minimizer.

<a id="a:eq-tilted-entropy"></a>
Suppose, then, that $x$ is such a minimizer. Take its positive maximizing law $p$ and a parameter $\theta$ from Lemma <a href="#ent:exponential" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ent:exponential">9</a>. For any $v\in\mathbb R^E$ and $t\in\mathbb R$, define $$p_a(t)=
  \frac{\exp((\theta+tv)\cdot a)}
       {\sum_{b\in\mathcal A_x}\exp((\theta+tv)\cdot b)},
  \qquad
  x(t)=\sum_{a\in\mathcal A_x}p_a(t)a.$$ The same lemma shows that $x(t)$ stays in the relative interior of $F_x$ and $$H(x(t))
  =\Phi(t)-(\theta+tv)\cdot x(t),
  \qquad
  \Phi(t)=\ln\sum_{a\in\mathcal A_x}
                       \exp((\theta+tv)\cdot a).
  \label{a:eq-tilted-entropy}$$ Thus we may differentiate along these curves without assuming differentiability of $H$ across faces.

<a id="a:eq-covariance-face-space"></a>
Let $$C=\sum_{a\in\mathcal A_x}p_a(a-x)(a-x)^{\mathsf T}$$ be the covariance matrix at $t=0$. Differentiating the finite sums gives $p_a'(0)=p_a\,v\cdot(a-x)$, hence $x'(0)=Cv$. For any $w\in\mathbb R^E$, $$w^{\mathsf T}Cw
  =\sum_{a\in\mathcal A_x}p_a\bigl(w\cdot(a-x)\bigr)^2.$$ All $p_a$ are positive, and $\operatorname{span}\{a-x:a\in\mathcal A_x\}=T(x)$. Since $C$ is symmetric, it follows that $$\ker C=T(x)^\perp,\qquad
  \operatorname{im}C=T(x),\qquad
  \operatorname{rank}C=d(x).
  \label{a:eq-covariance-face-space}$$ At a fractional mean, some coordinate has variance $C_{ee}=x_e(1-x_e)>0$, so $d(x)\ge1$. Also $x''(0)\in T(x)$, because the whole curve lies in $\operatorname{aff}F_x=x+T(x)$.

On the fractional coordinates, write $$g_e=-\ln x_e-c\ln(1-x_e)-(c+1),
  \qquad
  D_{ee}=-\frac1{x_e}+\frac c{1-x_e};$$ let $D$ be diagonal, and set $g_e=D_{ee}=0$ on the other coordinates. These entries represent the derivatives of $J_c$ restricted to the face. Indeed, a coordinate with $x_e\in\{0,1\}$ has that value on every face atom, so its contribution to $J_c(x(t))$ is constantly zero. A fractional coordinate takes both bit values among the face atoms; positivity of $p(t)$ keeps $0<x_e(t)<1$ for every finite $t$. Consequently the usual chain rule on fractional coordinates gives all derivatives of $J_c(x(t))$.

<a id="a:eq-entropy-second-variation"></a>
From $\Phi'(t)=v\cdot x(t)$ and Equation <a href="#a:eq-tilted-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq-tilted-entropy">[a:eq-tilted-entropy]</a>, we obtain $$\frac{d}{dt} H(x(t))
  =-(\theta+tv)\cdot x'(t).$$ Both signs of $t$ are permitted. The minimum property at $t=0$ therefore gives $$(\theta+g)\cdot Cv=0
  \qquad\text{for every }v\in\mathbb R^E.$$ By Equation <a href="#a:eq-covariance-face-space" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq-covariance-face-space">[a:eq-covariance-face-space]</a>, $\theta+g$ is orthogonal to $T(x)$, and in particular $(\theta+g)\cdot x''(0)=0$. The second derivative at the minimum is accordingly $$\begin{aligned}
  0
  &\le
    \left.\frac{d^2}{dt^2}
      \bigl( H(x(t))-J_c(x(t))\bigr)\right|_{t=0}\\
  &=-v^{\mathsf T}Cv-(\theta+g)\cdot x''(0)
                     -(Cv)^{\mathsf T}D(Cv)\\
  &=-v^{\mathsf T}Cv-(Cv)^{\mathsf T}D(Cv).
\end{aligned}
\label{a:eq-entropy-second-variation}$$

<a id="a:eq-entropy-trace-lower"></a>
Let $u_1,\ldots,u_d$ be orthonormal eigenvectors for the positive eigenvalues $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_d$ of $C$, where $d=d(x)$. Substituting $v=u_i$ in Equation <a href="#a:eq-entropy-second-variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq-entropy-second-variation">[a:eq-entropy-second-variation]</a> and dividing by $\lambda_i>0$ gives $\lambda_i u_i^{\mathsf T}(-D)u_i\ge1$. Summing and using $C=\sum_{i=1}^d\lambda_i u_i u_i^{\mathsf T}$, we find $$d(x)\le
  \sum_{i=1}^d\lambda_i u_i^{\mathsf T}(-D)u_i
  =\operatorname{tr}(C(-D)).
  \label{a:eq-entropy-trace-lower}$$ Only the spectral decomposition of $C$ is used here; no inverse of $C$, or simultaneous diagonalization of $C$ and $D$, is needed.

Each matching coordinate is Boolean, so $C_{ee}=x_e(1-x_e)$. Writing $s_f$ for the number of vertices incident with $E_f(x)$, Corollary <a href="#a:cor-fractional-dimension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:cor-fractional-dimension">31</a> gives <a id="a:eq-covariance-trace-c"></a>$$\label{a:eq-covariance-trace-c}
\begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{tr}(C(-D))
 &=\sum_{e\in E_f(x)}x_e(1-x_e)
       \left(\frac1{x_e}-\frac c{1-x_e}\right)\\
 &=|E_f(x)|-(c+1)\sum_{e\in E_f(x)}x_e
   =|E_f(x)|-\frac{c+1}{2}s_f.
\end{aligned}$$ For $c=4$, the weaker consequence $d(x)\ge|E_f(x)|-2s_f$ of the residual dimension bound suffices: $$\operatorname{tr}(C(-D))=|E_f(x)|-\tfrac52s_f
 <|E_f(x)|-2s_f\le d(x),$$ because a fractional point has $s_f>0$. For $c=3$, the trace is $|E_f(x)|-2s_f$, so we use the exact residual cases. If $s_f\ge4$, then $$d(x)\ge|E_f(x)|-2(s_f-2)
       =|E_f(x)|-2s_f+4
       >\operatorname{tr}(C(-D)).$$ If $s_f=2$, then $$d(x)\ge|E_f(x)|-1>|E_f(x)|-4
       =\operatorname{tr}(C(-D)).$$ These exhaust the positive even values of $s_f$. For either coefficient, the result contradicts Equation <a href="#a:eq-entropy-trace-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq-entropy-trace-lower">[a:eq-entropy-trace-lower]</a>. Thus no fractional minimizer exists; the minimum is attained at an integral mean and equals zero. This proves the lower bound for each coefficient by its own covariance contradiction. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### Two other entropy comparisons

The covariance comparisons just proved give separable counting estimates and a short scalar proof of the nonlinear bound. We give those deductions here before the distinct normalized-covariance proof in Appendix <a href="#b:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="b:section">12</a>. As in Section <a href="#cons:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cons:section">4</a>, $N(G)$ denotes the number of labelled perfect matchings, and $B(x)\le m$ by Equation <a href="#cons:B-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cons:B-bound">[cons:B-bound]</a>.

<a id="stmt-cons-legacy-defect"></a>
<div id="cons:legacy-defect" class="corollary">

**Corollary 33** (Separable and nonlinear consequences). *For every $x\in P(G)$ with $|V(G)|=n=2m>0$, <a id="cons:legacy-separable"></a><a id="cons:legacy-nonlinear"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 F(x)-4B(x)&\le F(x)-3B(x)\le H(x)\le F(x)+B(x),\label{cons:legacy-separable}\\
 F(x)-H(x)&\le\min\{F(x),3m\}
                  \le8m\bigl(1-e^{-F(x)/m}\bigr).\label{cons:legacy-nonlinear}
\end{aligned}$$ Furthermore, <a id="cons:legacy-bin-gap"></a>$$\label{cons:legacy-bin-gap}
 \ln N(G)\le\max_{x\in P(G)}[F(x)+B(x)]
       \le\ln N(G)+2n
       \le\ln N(G)+\tfrac52n.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cons-legacy-defect"><summary>Proof of stmt-cons-legacy-defect</summary>

*Proof.* Theorem <a href="#a:thm-separable" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:thm-separable">32</a> proves $F-3B\le H\le F+B$ by the contraction and covariance route; its coefficient-four proof gives $F-4B\le H$ as well. Since $B\ge0$, the first two terms are ordered as displayed. Nonnegativity of entropy and $B\le m$ give $F-H\le\min\{F,3m\}$. Put $t=F(x)/m\ge0$ and $f(t)=8(1-e^{-t})$. On $[0,\ln2]$, one has $f(0)=0$ and $f'(t)=8e^{-t}\ge4\ge1$, hence $f(t)\ge t$. For $t\ge\ln2$, one has $f(t)\ge4\ge3$. Thus $\min\{t,3\}\le f(t)$, proving the nonlinear inequality including $t=0$. This deduction is independent of the normalized inverse-covariance proof of Theorem <a href="#b:thm-nonlinear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="b:thm-nonlinear">36</a>.

For the optimized bound, $H\le\ln N$ and $(F+B)-(F-3B)=4B\le4m=2n$ give the upper error $2n$. Using $F-4B$ instead gives the separate original comparison $(F+B)-(F-4B)=5B\le5m=5n/2$. The uniform matching law supplies the lower bound exactly as in Corollary <a href="#cons:entropy-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cons:entropy-count">13</a>. The sharper error $3m-2$ in that corollary improves both comparisons on the same domain. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### Structural estimates from the other entropy bounds

For $k\ge1$, put $b_1=1$ and $b_k=k(1-1/k)^{3(k-1)}$ for $k\ge2$.

<a id="stmt-cons-legacy-regular"></a>
<div id="cons:legacy-regular" class="corollary">

**Corollary 34** (Coefficient-three and nonlinear counts). *<span id="cons:legacy-counts" label="cons:legacy-counts"></span> A loopless labelled multigraph $G$ on $2m>0$ vertices containing $k\ge1$ pairwise edge-disjoint perfect matchings satisfies <a id="cons:legacy-three-count"></a><a id="cons:legacy-eight-count"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 N(G)&\ge b_k^m\ge(e^{-3}k)^m,\label{cons:legacy-three-count}\\
 N(G)&\ge\bigl[k\exp\{-8(1-1/k)\}\bigr]^m
                         \ge(e^{-8}k)^m.\label{cons:legacy-eight-count}
\end{aligned}$$ The same two conclusions hold when $G$ is $k$-regular and every odd cut has at least $k$ edges.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cons-legacy-regular"><summary>Proof of stmt-cons-legacy-regular</summary>

*Proof.* For the selected matching mixture in Theorem <a href="#cons:structural" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cons:structural">14</a>, $F(x)=m\ln k$ and $x$ vanishes outside their labelled union. For $k\ge2$, $F(x)-3B(x)=m\ln k+3m(k-1)\ln(1-1/k)$. Using $H(x)\le\ln N(G)$ and the coefficient-three inequality in Corollary <a href="#cons:legacy-defect" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cons:legacy-defect">33</a>, then exponentiating, gives the first bound. The inequality $(k-1)\ln(1-1/k)\ge-1$ from the proof of Theorem <a href="#cons:structural" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cons:structural">14</a> gives its coarse form. At $k=1$ the selected matching gives $N(G)\ge1=b_1^m$.

At this same mean, the nonlinear inequality gives $\ln N(G)\ge m\ln k-8m(1-1/k)$, including $k=1$. Exponentiating and using $1-1/k\le1$ proves the second line. For a regular graph satisfying the odd-cut hypothesis, Corollary <a href="#cons:regular" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cons:regular">15</a> proves that the uniform vector is feasible; the same evaluations then prove both statements without requiring a partition of the edges into perfect matchings. Its cut-parity argument also proves the $(k-1)$-edge-connected even-order case. ◻

</details>

For $k\ge2$, the finite-order bound of Theorem <a href="#cons:structural" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cons:structural">14</a> implies the coefficient-three bound directly, since $0<1-1/k<1$ and $2(m-1)(k-1)\le3m(k-1)$. The coefficient-three logarithmic loss is at most $3$, while $8(1-1/k)\ge4$ for $k\ge2$, so it also dominates the second displayed base. We have nevertheless derived that base from the nonlinear inequality to retain the distinct implication. At $k=1$, the refined bounds $b_1^m$ and $[k\exp\{-8(1-1/k)\}]^m$ both equal one.

<a id="stmt-cons-legacy-partition"></a>
<div id="cons:legacy-partition" class="corollary">

**Corollary 35** (An exact partition of the ambient edges). *If the entire edge set of a loopless labelled graph on $2m>0$ vertices is partitioned by $k\ge1$ perfect matchings, then $$N(G)\ge\bigl[k\exp\{-8(1-1/k)\}\bigr]^m\ge(e^{-8}k)^m.$$ When $k=1$ the count is exactly one. If $k=0$ and the order is positive, the graph is edgeless and its count is zero; only the coarse bound is then asserted. On the empty vertex set there is one empty matching.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cons-legacy-partition"><summary>Proof of stmt-cons-legacy-partition</summary>

*Proof.* The partition provides the hypothesis of Corollary <a href="#cons:legacy-counts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cons:legacy-counts">[cons:legacy-counts]</a>; its uniform law has marginal $1/k$ on every ambient edge. Thus the nonlinear proof just given applies verbatim. For $k=1$ the entire graph is a single perfect matching. For $k=0$ it has no edges. The empty case follows directly from the definition of a matching and does not use expressions containing $1/m$. ◻

</details>

The exact-partition hypothesis in the last corollary is stronger than containing a selected union. In particular, a graph containing one perfect matching may have more than one, whereas the exact one-matching union has count one.

<a id="b:section"></a>
### A normalized-covariance proof of the nonlinear entropy bound

We give a direct proof of the entropy-dependent estimate using the supported-face codimension bound from Proposition <a href="#a:prop-supported-codimension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop-supported-codimension">30</a>. The normalization below makes the marginal-entropy Hessian equal to minus the identity, while the covariance trace has an exact combinatorial value. These two identities lead to a contradiction at any positive maximum of the proposed entropy defect. The scalar deduction in Corollary <a href="#cons:legacy-defect" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cons:legacy-defect">33</a> is a separate proof of the same bound.

Throughout this appendix, $G=(V,E)$ is a finite loopless multigraph on $2m>0$ vertices with at least one perfect matching. Parallel edges have distinct labels. Write $\mathcal M$ for its perfect matchings, $a(M)=\mathbf1_M$, and $P=P(G)=\operatorname{conv}\{a(M):M\in\mathcal M\}$. Recall that, with natural logarithms and $0\ln0=0$, $$F(y)=-\sum_{e\in E}y_e\ln y_e,
 \qquad
 H(y)=\max_{\substack{p\text{ a probability law on }\mathcal M\\
                       \sum_Mp_Ma(M)=y}}
                   \left(-\sum_{M\in\mathcal M}p_M\ln p_M\right).$$ To distinguish entropy of a law from its maximum at a prescribed mean, write $\operatorname{Ent}(p)=-\sum_Mp_M\ln p_M$.

<a id="stmt-b-thm-nonlinear"></a>
<div id="b:thm-nonlinear" class="theorem">

**Theorem 36** (Nonlinear entropy bound). *For every $y\in P(G)$, <a id="b:eq-nonlinear"></a>$$F(y)-H(y)\le 8m\bigl(1-e^{-F(y)/m}\bigr).
 \label{b:eq-nonlinear}$$*

</div>

The attainment and continuity of $H$ on the whole polytope follow from Lemma <a href="#ent:continuity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ent:continuity">8</a>. In particular, a continuous defect has a maximum even when that maximum lies on a proper face. The proof below combines the codimension bound with that lemma, the face description, and the exponential law of Lemma <a href="#ent:exponential" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ent:exponential">9</a>. These inputs are independent of Theorem <a href="#ent:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ent:main">1</a>.

<a id="b:positive-maximum"></a>
#### The face at a hypothetical positive maximum

<details id="proof-stmt-b-thm-nonlinear"><summary>Proof of stmt-b-thm-nonlinear</summary>

<a id="b:eq-large-dimension"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#b:thm-nonlinear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="b:thm-nonlinear">36</a>.* Define $$f(t)=8(1-e^{-t}),
 \qquad
 \Psi(y)=F(y)-H(y)-mf\bigl(F(y)/m\bigr).$$ Suppose that $\Psi$ is positive somewhere. By Lemma <a href="#ent:continuity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ent:continuity">8</a>, it has a positive maximum at some $x\in P$. Let $F_x$ be the minimal face containing $x$, and put $$A=\{e\in E:x_e>0\},\qquad s=|A|,\qquad
 d=\dim F_x,\qquad t=F(x)/m.$$ Here $s$ counts supported *edges*. Each matching has exactly $m$ edges, so $\sum_ex_e=m$ and $0\le x_e\le1$. Concavity of $-u\ln u$ gives $$0\le t\le\ln(s/m).$$ On $[0,\ln8]$, we have $f(0)=0$ and $f'(t)\ge1$, so $f(t)\ge t$. Since $H(x)\ge0$, positivity of $\Psi(x)$ therefore forces $t>\ln8$, and hence $s>8m$. The supported-face estimate of Proposition <a href="#a:prop-supported-codimension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop-supported-codimension">30</a> now yields $$d\ge s-4m>\frac{s}{2}>0.
 \label{b:eq-large-dimension}$$ Thus all integral and low-entropy cases have already been excluded before any covariance inverse is introduced.

Let $$\mathcal M_x=\{M\in\mathcal M:a(M)\in F_x\}.$$ Every law whose mean is in $F_x$ is supported on $\mathcal M_x$. Indeed, each inequality active on this face has nonnegative slack at every matching vector. Expected slack zero forces every atom of positive probability to satisfy its equality. This applies to both zero coordinates and tight odd cuts. Consequently $F_x=\operatorname{conv}\{a(M):M\in\mathcal M_x\}$.

<a id="b:eq-normalization"></a>
Discard the coordinates outside $A$ and set $$D=\operatorname{diag}(x_e:e\in A),\qquad
 \mathcal T=D^{-1/2}T(x)
     =\operatorname{span}\bigl(D^{-1/2}(F_x-x)\bigr)
       \subseteq\mathbb R^A.
 \label{b:eq-normalization}$$ All diagonal entries of $D$ are positive. By Lemma <a href="#geom:lem-face" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geom:lem-face">5</a>, $x$ is relatively interior to $F_x$. Therefore $$z=D^{-1/2}(y-x),\qquad y=x+D^{1/2}z$$ identifies a relative neighborhood of $x$ in the face with a neighborhood of zero in the $d$-dimensional Euclidean space $\mathcal T$. Define $$b_M=D^{-1/2}(a(M)-x),\qquad M\in\mathcal M_x.$$ Their convex hull contains a neighborhood of zero in $\mathcal T$. All gradients, Hessians, operator inverses, and operator traces in the remainder of the proof are taken on $\mathcal T$. Coordinates with $x_e=1$ have not been discarded: their centered entries and all tangent directions are zero, so they cause no singularity on this space.

<a id="b:exponential-family"></a>
#### A finite exponential family on the normalized tangent space

We now express the face law of Lemma <a href="#ent:exponential" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ent:exponential">9</a> in the normalized coordinates. Let $p$ be the positive maximizing law at $x$, and write its parameter as $\theta\in\mathbb R^A$, restricting to supported coordinates. Since $a(M)=x+D^{1/2}b_M$, $$\theta\cdot a(M)=\theta\cdot x+
                      (D^{1/2}\theta)\cdot b_M.$$ Every $b_M$ lies in $\mathcal T$. Thus only the orthogonal projection $\lambda=\operatorname{proj}_{\mathcal T}
(D^{1/2}\theta)$ affects the normalized law. Define $$\Phi(\eta)=\ln\sum_{M\in\mathcal M_x}e^{\eta\cdot b_M}
 \qquad(\eta\in\mathcal T).$$ The law and its normalized mean then satisfy <a id="b:eq-exponential-law"></a>$$p_M=\exp\bigl(\lambda\cdot b_M-\Phi(\lambda)\bigr),
 \qquad \nabla\Phi(\lambda)=0.
 \label{b:eq-exponential-law}$$ The last equality is the zero mean of $b_M$ under $p$. The parameter is finite because the original law is taken on its minimal face.

<a id="b:eq-mean-entropy-covariance"></a>
For any $\eta\in\mathcal T$, put $$p_{\eta,M}=e^{\eta\cdot b_M-\Phi(\eta)},\qquad
 z(\eta)=\sum_Mp_{\eta,M}b_M,\qquad
 y(\eta)=x+D^{1/2}z(\eta).$$ This law has exponential parameter $D^{-1/2}\eta$ in the original coordinates, since the centering term is constant across atoms. Lemma <a href="#ent:exponential" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ent:exponential">9</a> therefore shows that it maximizes entropy at its own mean $y(\eta)$ and remains on the same minimal face. Differentiating the finite sum defining $\Phi$ yields $$\begin{aligned}
 z(\eta)&=\nabla\Phi(\eta),\\
 H(y(\eta))&=\Phi(\eta)-\eta\cdot z(\eta),\\
 C(\eta)&:=\nabla^2\Phi(\eta)
       =\operatorname{Cov}_{p_\eta}(b_M).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{b:eq-mean-entropy-covariance}$$ Each $C(\eta)$ is positive definite on $\mathcal T$. Indeed, zero variance in a direction $u\in\mathcal T$ would make $u\cdot b_M$ constant on all face atoms because $p_\eta$ is positive. The affine span of the $b_M$ is $\mathcal T$, so this forces $u=0$. Write $C=C(\lambda)$.

<a id="b:second-variation"></a>
#### The second variation and its trace

We have a positive definite covariance on a space of dimension $d>s/2$. We now compare its inverse with the marginal-entropy Hessian. This comparison uses the smooth exponential parameter; it does not require differentiability of $H$ across faces.

<a id="b:eq-hessian-inequality"></a>
Set $$\widehat F(z)=F(x+D^{1/2}z),\qquad
 L(z)=\widehat F(z)-mf\bigl(\widehat F(z)/m\bigr).$$ These functions are smooth near zero on $\mathcal T$. In the retained coordinates, the Hessian of $F$ at $x$ is $-D^{-1}$. The change of coordinates in Equation <a href="#b:eq-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="b:eq-normalization">[b:eq-normalization]</a> therefore gives <a id="b:eq-normalized-hessian"></a>$$\nabla^2\widehat F(0)=-I_{\mathcal T}.
 \label{b:eq-normalized-hessian}$$ By Equation <a href="#b:eq-mean-entropy-covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="b:eq-mean-entropy-covariance">[b:eq-mean-entropy-covariance]</a>, the smooth function $$J(\eta)=L(z(\eta))-\Phi(\eta)+\eta\cdot z(\eta)
        =\Psi(y(\eta))$$ has a local maximum at $\lambda$. Its gradient is $$\nabla J(\eta)
   =C(\eta)\bigl(\nabla L(z(\eta))+\eta\bigr).$$ Since $C$ is invertible and $z(\lambda)=0$, stationarity implies $\nabla L(0)+\lambda=0$. Differentiating once more at $\lambda$, the derivative of $C(\eta)$ is multiplied by this zero vector. Thus the second-derivative test gives $$\nabla^2J(\lambda)=C\nabla^2L(0)C+C\preceq0.$$ Congruence by $C^{-1}$, as an operator on $\mathcal T$, yields $$\nabla^2L(0)+C^{-1}\preceq0.
 \label{b:eq-hessian-inequality}$$

<a id="b:eq-inverse-trace"></a>
Under $p=p_\lambda$, the normalized vector $b_M$ has mean zero. For each edge $e$, its indicator in the random matching has variance $x_e(1-x_e)$, so <a id="b:eq-covariance-trace"></a>$$\operatorname{tr}_{\mathcal T}C
 =\mathbb E_p\|b_M\|_2^2
 =\sum_{e\in A}\frac{x_e(1-x_e)}{x_e}
 =s-m.
 \label{b:eq-covariance-trace}$$ The trace on $\mathcal T$ equals the ambient trace because all the vectors $b_M$ lie in $\mathcal T$. For $u>0$, the inequality $u^{-1}\ge2-u$ follows from $(u-1)^2/u\ge0$. Applying it to the $d$ positive eigenvalues of $C$ gives $$\begin{split}
 \operatorname{tr}_{\mathcal T}(-I_{\mathcal T}+C^{-1})
 &\ge d-\operatorname{tr}_{\mathcal T}C\\
 &=d-s+m\ge-3m.
 \end{split}
 \label{b:eq-inverse-trace}$$ The last inequality is again the supported-face codimension bound.

<a id="b:eq-rank-one-correction"></a>
It remains to include the nonlinear correction. Writing $g=\nabla\widehat F(0)$, the full chain rule is $$\left.\nabla^2\!\left[mf\bigl(\widehat F(z)/m\bigr)\right]
       \right|_{z=0}
 =-f'(t)I_{\mathcal T}+\frac{f''(t)}{m}gg^{\mathsf T}.
 \label{b:eq-rank-one-correction}$$ In particular, the rank-one term has trace $f''(t)\|g\|_2^2/m\le0$. Since $t\le\ln(s/m)$, we also have $f'(t)=8e^{-t}\ge8m/s$. Combining Equations <a href="#b:eq-large-dimension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="b:eq-large-dimension">[b:eq-large-dimension]</a>, <a href="#b:eq-inverse-trace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="b:eq-inverse-trace">[b:eq-inverse-trace]</a>, and <a href="#b:eq-rank-one-correction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="b:eq-rank-one-correction">[b:eq-rank-one-correction]</a>, we obtain <a id="b:eq-strict-contradiction"></a>$$\begin{split}
 \operatorname{tr}_{\mathcal T}\bigl(\nabla^2L(0)+C^{-1}\bigr)
 &\ge-3m+f'(t)d\\
 &\ge-3m+\frac{8md}{s}>m>0.
 \end{split}
 \label{b:eq-strict-contradiction}$$ This contradicts the negative semidefiniteness in Equation <a href="#b:eq-hessian-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="b:eq-hessian-inequality">[b:eq-hessian-inequality]</a>. Hence $\Psi(y)\le0$ throughout $P$, proving Theorem <a href="#b:thm-nonlinear" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="b:thm-nonlinear">36</a>. ◻

</details>

The theorem assumes $m>0$ because its formula divides by $m$. On the empty vertex set, the unique empty matching has $F=H=0$; its entropy statement is handled separately without that quotient. If a nonempty graph has no perfect matching, there are no means in $P(G)$ to which the theorem applies.

<a id="c:app-loops"></a>
### A convention allowing singleton loops

Suppose a loop is allowed to cover its single incident vertex. Let $\ell_i$ be the number of distinguished loop choices at vertex $i$, and retain the multiplicities $k_{\{i,j\}}$ for pairs of distinct vertices. All these integers are given in binary. A matching under this convention covers every vertex exactly once by either a chosen loop or an ordinary pair. Write $N_j$ for the number of such matchings using exactly $j$ loops, and $N_{\mathrm{loop}}=\sum_{j=0}^n N_j$.

<a id="stmt-c-prop-loops"></a>
<div id="c:prop-loops" class="proposition">

**Proposition 37**. *There is one deterministic algorithm which, given a graph on $n$ vertices with nonnegative binary integer pair and singleton-loop multiplicities, returns a nonnegative rational number $A_{\mathrm{loop}}$ such that $$\frac{N_{\mathrm{loop}}}{2^{18n}}
                  \le A_{\mathrm{loop}}\le N_{\mathrm{loop}}.$$ It returns zero exactly when $N_{\mathrm{loop}}=0$. Its running time and output length are polynomial in the input bit length, including when the input gives $n$ in binary and lists only the positive pair and loop records.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-c-prop-loops"><summary>Proof of stmt-c-prop-loops</summary>

*Proof.* First count the distinct vertices occurring in a positive ordinary pair or having a positive loop count. If any of the $n$ vertices is missing, it cannot be covered, so return zero. This test uses only the listed records, without enumerating the vertex set. Otherwise, if there are $r$ positive pair records and $s$ positive loop records, then $n\le2r+s$. Thus the constructions indexed by vertices below have size polynomial in the input length, even for a compact binary vertex count.

For each $j\in\{0,\ldots,n\}$, construct an ordinary loopless graph $G_j$ by adjoining $j$ labeled new vertices. Keep all original edges between distinct vertices. There are no edges among the new vertices, and the pair joining original vertex $i$ to any new vertex has multiplicity $\ell_i$. Label these parallel edges by the corresponding original loop choices.

<a id="c:eq-loop-count-identity"></a>
Every perfect matching of $G_j$ pairs the new vertices to $j$ distinct original vertices. Replace those pairs by their labeled loop choices and retain the other edges; this gives a matching counted by $N_j$. Conversely, each matching counted by $N_j$ has exactly $j!$ lifts, one for each bijection from the new vertices to its $j$ loop-covered vertices. Edge labels preserve the loop choices independently of this bijection. Thus, including $j=0$, $$\label{c:eq-loop-count-identity}
                         N(G_j)=j!\,N_j.$$

Run the ordinary algorithm on each $G_j$, obtaining a nonnegative integer $A_j$, and return <a id="c:eq-loop-estimate"></a>$$\label{c:eq-loop-estimate}
                         A_{\mathrm{loop}}
                              =\sum_{j=0}^n\frac{A_j}{j!}.$$ Parity is tested on each transformed order $n+j$, as part of that ordinary algorithm. There is no initial rejection for odd $n$: a single vertex with a loop is already a positive-count example. The parity of $n+j$ equals that of the number $n-j$ of original vertices that remain to be paired by ordinary edges.

By Theorem <a href="#thm:algorithm-guarantee" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:algorithm-guarantee">2</a> and Equation <a href="#c:eq-loop-count-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="c:eq-loop-count-identity">[c:eq-loop-count-identity]</a>, $$\frac{N_j}{512^{\,n+j}}
                   \le\frac{A_j}{j!}\le N_j.$$ Since $n+j\le2n$, summing these inequalities proves the claimed factor $512^{2n}=2^{18n}$. All terms are nonnegative, and $A_j=0$ exactly when $N_j=0$, so zero recognition is also exact.

The construction retains compressed multiplicity records: $G_j$ has at most $r+js$ positive pair records. No parallel-edge list is expanded. Each transformed graph has at most $2n$ vertices and uses only multiplicities already present in the input. If $b_{\max}$ is the largest binary exponent of any positive pair or loop multiplicity, taking $b_{\max}=0$ when all multiplicities are zero, Proposition <a href="#d:prop-bit-complexity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="d:prop-bit-complexity">28</a> bounds each call and gives $O(n^2+nb_{\max}+1)$ bits for $A_j$. There are only $n+1$ calls. The rational sum can be represented exactly with the common denominator $n!$: <a id="c:eq-loop-common-denominator"></a>$$\label{c:eq-loop-common-denominator}
       A_{\mathrm{loop}}
          =\frac{\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^n
                              A_j\,\frac{n!}{j!}}{n!}.$$ The denominator has $O(n\log_2(n+1)+1)$ bits, and the numerator has $O(n^2+nb_{\max}+n\log_2(n+1)+1)$ bits. Factorials, exact integer divisions, products, and the sum therefore take polynomial bit time. The case $n=0$ uses the single term $j=0$, with $0!=1$ and $A_0=1$, so the empty matching is counted exactly. ◻

</details>

      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  x=1.05cm,y=1.05cm,\n  matched/.style={very thick,blue!75!black},\n  vertex/.style={circle,fill,inner sep=1.4pt},\n  every node/.style={font=\\small}\n]\n  \\coordinate (v) at (0,0);\n  \\coordinate (u1) at (-1.25,0);\n  \\coordinate (u2) at (0.85,0.9);\n  \\coordinate (u3) at (0.85,-0.9);\n  \\draw[matched] (u1)--node[above] {\\(e_1\\)} (v);\n  \\draw (u2)--node[above left] {\\(e_2\\)} (v);\n  \\draw (u3)--node[below left] {\\(e_3\\)} (v);\n  \\node[vertex,label=below left:\\(v\\)] at (v) {};\n  \\node[vertex,label=left:\\(u_1\\)] at (u1) {};\n  \\node[vertex,label=above:\\(u_2\\)] at (u2) {};\n  \\node[vertex,label=below:\\(u_3\\)] at (u3) {};\n\n  \\draw[->] (1.35,0)--node[above] {expand} (2.25,0);\n\n  \\coordinate (t1) at (4,0);\n  \\coordinate (t2) at (5.2,0.7);\n  \\coordinate (t3) at (5.2,-0.7);\n  \\coordinate (w1) at (2.75,0);\n  \\coordinate (w2) at (6.35,1.1);\n  \\coordinate (w3) at (6.35,-1.1);\n  \\draw[matched] (w1)--node[above] {\\(e_1\\)} (t1);\n  \\draw (w2)--node[above] {\\(e_2\\)} (t2);\n  \\draw (w3)--node[below] {\\(e_3\\)} (t3);\n  \\draw (t1)--(t2) (t1)--(t3);\n  \\draw[matched] (t2)--node[right] {\\(f_1\\)} (t3);\n  \\node[vertex,label=below left:\\(t_1\\)] at (t1) {};\n  \\node[vertex,label=above left:\\(t_2\\)] at (t2) {};\n  \\node[vertex,label=below left:\\(t_3\\)] at (t3) {};\n  \\node[vertex,label=below:\\(u_1\\)] at (w1) {};\n  \\node[vertex,label=above:\\(u_2\\)] at (w2) {};\n  \\node[vertex,label=below:\\(u_3\\)] at (w3) {};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A local graph transformation. On the left, a central vertex $v$ is connected to vertices $u_1, u_2, u_3$ via edges $e_1, e_2, e_3$, where $e_1$ is drawn as a thick blue line and $e_2, e_3$ are thin black lines. A horizontal arrow labelled \"expand\" points to the right configuration. On the right, vertex $v$ is expanded into a triangle with vertices $t_1, t_2, t_3$, connected to $u_1, u_2, u_3$ by edges $e_1, e_2, e_3$, with $e_1$ shown as a thick blue line. Inside the triangle, the edge opposite to $t_1$ between $t_2$ and $t_3$ is labelled $f_1$ and shown as a thick blue line; the other two edges of the triangle ($t_1 t_2$ and $t_1 t_3$) are thin black lines."

    {
      "caption": "Lifting through an even-terminal contraction. Filled dots are terminals and the open dot represents the contracted block. In this schematic instance each side initially has two terminals. The shaded child side lifts to the shaded parent side by taking the entire contracted block, preserving odd parity. Parallel crossing capacities are added, so the cut capacity is preserved as well.",
      "figure_id": "d:fig-odd-cut-lift",
      "pdf_page": 28,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "d:fig-odd-cut-lift",
          "exact_quote": "Lifting through an even-terminal contraction.\nFilled dots are terminals and the open dot represents the contracted\nblock.  In this schematic instance each side initially has two terminals.\nThe shaded child side lifts to the shaded parent si",
          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  x=1cm,y=1cm,\n  terminal/.style={circle,fill=black,inner sep=2.3pt},\n  contracted/.style={circle,draw=black,fill=white,line width=.7pt,inner sep=3pt},\n  block/.style={draw=black!55,rounded corners=3pt},\n  cutshade/.style={fill=blue!12,draw=none},\n  edge/.style={draw=black!50,line width=.55pt},\n  every node/.style={font=\\small}\n]\n\\path[cutshade] (0.25,1.3)--(2.55,1.3)--(2.55,.2)--(4.55,.2)\n               --(4.55,2.25)--(.25,2.25)--cycle;\n\\draw[block] (.15,.1) rectangle (2.25,2.35);\n\\draw[block] (2.45,.1) rectangle (4.65,2.35);\n\\node at (1.2,2.68) {$A$};\n\\node at (3.55,2.68) {$\\overline A$};\n\\node[terminal] (a1) at (1.2,1.8) {};\n\\node[terminal] (a2) at (1.2,.65) {};\n\\node[terminal] (b1) at (3.55,1.8) {};\n\\node[terminal] (b2) at (3.55,.65) {};\n\\draw[edge] (a1)--(a2);\n\\draw[edge] (a1)--(b1);\n\\draw[edge] (a1)--(b2);\n\\draw[edge] (a2)--(b1);\n\\draw[edge] (a2)--(b2);\n\\draw[edge] (b1)--(b2);\n\\node at (2.4,-.3) {lifted side: three terminals};\n\n\\draw[-{Stealth[length=2mm]},line width=.7pt]\n  (4.95,1.2)--(6.35,1.2);\n\\node[align=center,font=\\footnotesize] at (5.65,1.8)\n  {contract\\\\$\\overline A$};\n\n\\path[cutshade] (6.7,1.3) rectangle (10.8,2.25);\n\\draw[block] (6.6,.1) rectangle (8.7,2.35);\n\\node at (7.65,2.68) {$A$};\n\\node[terminal] (c1) at (7.65,1.8) {};\n\\node[terminal] (c2) at (7.65,.65) {};\n\\node[contracted] (z) at (10.2,1.8) {};\n\\draw[edge] (c1)--(c2);\n\\draw[edge] (c1)--(z);\n\\draw[edge] (c2)--(z);\n\\node[align=center,font=\\footnotesize] at (10.2,.7)\n  {nonterminal\\\\even number of\\\\original terminals};\n\\node at (8.7,-.3) {child side: one terminal};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Schematic diagram showing lifting through contraction. On the left side (\"lifted side: three terminals\"), a box is partitioned into a shaded region labelled $A$ and an unshaded region labelled $\\overline{A}$. It contains four terminal vertices (filled black dots) connected by crossing edges and a vertical edge; three terminals fall within shaded region $A$ and one terminal lies in $\\overline{A}$. A right-pointing arrow between the boxes is labelled \"contract\" above and \"$\\overline{A}$\" below. On the right side (\"child side: one terminal\"), a box contains two filled black terminal dots on the left and an open circular dot on the right representing the contracted block. An arrow points to the open dot with labels \"nonterminal\" and \"even number of original terminals\". Shaded region $A$ at the top left encloses one terminal."
    }
